A force F = 6t² N acts for 2 s on a 2 kg particle at rest. Find its final speed.

1. I = ∫₀² 6t² dt = 16 N s. v = 16/2 = 8 m s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Ignoring direction: a ball rebounding changes momentum by m(u + v), not m(v − u) with both positive.

### MB1 Conservation of momentum and restitution

*Refs: AQA MB1, MB2 · Edexcel FM1 4.1, 4.2 · OCR 6.03b, 6.03d, 6.03i, 6.03j, 6.03k · MEI Mi4, Mi5, Mi6, Mi7, Mi8, Mi9, Mi10, Mi11, Mi12. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- In a collision with no external impulse, total momentum is conserved.
- Newton's experimental law: speed of separation = e × speed of approach, 0 ≤ e ≤ 1. For a wall: rebound speed = e × approach speed.
- e = 1: perfectly elastic (kinetic energy conserved). e = 0: perfectly inelastic (the bodies coalesce or move together).
- Kinetic energy is lost when e < 1.

#### Explained

Draw before and after diagrams, choose a positive direction, and write two equations: momentum, and restitution as v₂ − v₁ = e(u₁ − u₂) (with A on the left). Solve them together. If an answer is negative, that sphere moves the other way.

#### Worked example

Sphere A (2 kg) at 5 m s⁻¹ hits sphere B (3 kg) moving towards it at 2 m s⁻¹. e = 0.5. Find the velocities after impact and the KE lost.

1. Momentum: 2(5) + 3(−2) = 2v₁ + 3v₂, so 2v₁ + 3v₂ = 4.
2. Restitution: v₂ − v₁ = 0.5(5 − (−2)) = 3.5.
3. v₁ = −1.3, v₂ = 2.2. A reverses at 1.3 m s⁻¹; B moves at 2.2 m s⁻¹.
4. KE before 25 + 6 = 31 J; after 1.69 + 7.26 = 8.95 J. Lost 22.05 J.

A ball dropped from 2.5 m bounces with e = 0.6. Find the height of the first bounce and the total distance travelled before it stops.

1. Impact speed √(2g × 2.5) = 7 m s⁻¹; rebound 4.2 m s⁻¹; height 4.2²/2g = 0.9 m = e² × 2.5.
2. Heights form a geometric series: total = 2.5 + 2(0.9 + 0.324 + ...) = 2.5 + 2 × 0.9/(1 − 0.36) = 5.31 m.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing the restitution equation with mixed signs.

### Successive impacts

*Refs: Edexcel FM1 4.2, 5.2 · OCR 6.03k · MEI Mi10. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Treat each collision separately, using velocities from the previous one. Decide whether a further collision happens by comparing velocities (a faster sphere behind a slower one moving the same way will catch it).

#### Worked example

Three spheres A, B, C of masses 1, 1, 2 kg lie in a line. A moves at 6 m s⁻¹ to hit B; then B hits C. e = 0.5 throughout. Find all velocities after the second collision and decide whether there is a third.

1. A–B: v_A + v_B = 6, v_B − v_A = 3. v_A = 1.5, v_B = 4.5.
2. B–C: v_B′ + 2v_C = 4.5, v_C − v_B′ = 2.25. v_C = 2.25, v_B′ = 0.
3. A (1.5) is now faster than B (0) in the same direction, so A hits B again.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the original velocities for the second collision.

### Oblique impacts

*Refs: Edexcel FM1 5.1, 5.2 · OCR 6.03l · MEI Mi13, Mi14, Mi15, Mi16, Mi17. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- For smooth surfaces, the impulse acts along the common normal (the line of centres for spheres). The velocity component perpendicular to the normal (parallel to the wall) is unchanged.
- Apply momentum conservation and Newton's law along the line of centres only.

#### Worked example

A ball hits a smooth wall at 6 m s⁻¹ at 30° to the wall. e = 0.5. Find the rebound speed and angle.

1. Parallel to wall: 6 cos 30° = 5.196 (unchanged). Perpendicular: 6 sin 30° = 3, becomes 1.5.
2. Speed √(5.196² + 1.5²) = 5.41 m s⁻¹, at arctan(1.5/5.196) = 16.1° to the wall.

Sphere A (1 kg) with velocity (3i + 4j) m s⁻¹ hits sphere B (2 kg) at rest. At impact the line of centres is parallel to i. e = ½. Find the velocities after impact and the KE lost.

1. j-components unchanged: A keeps 4j, B has 0j.
2. Along i: 3 = v₁ + 2v₂ and v₂ − v₁ = 1.5. v₁ = 0, v₂ = 1.5.
3. A: 4j m s⁻¹; B: 1.5i m s⁻¹.
4. KE before 12.5 J; after 8 + 2.25 = 10.25 J. Lost 2.25 J.

#### Common mistakes

- Applying restitution to the full speeds rather than the normal components.

### Check questions

1. A 1500 kg car moving at 12 m s⁻¹ collides with a stationary 1000 kg car and they move together. Find their common speed and the impulse on the second car. [4 marks]
2. Sphere P (3 kg, 4 m s⁻¹) hits sphere Q (1 kg, at rest). After impact Q moves at 4.5 m s⁻¹. Find P's velocity and e. [5 marks]
3. A ball falls from 3.6 m onto a floor and rebounds to 1.6 m. Find e. [3 marks]
4. A smooth sphere moving at 10 m s⁻¹ hits a smooth wall at 60° to the wall. After the impact it moves at 45° to the wall. Find e and the loss of KE as a fraction of the original. [6 marks]
5. A force F = (2t i + 3 j) N acts on a 0.5 kg particle from t = 0 to t = 2, starting with velocity (i − j) m s⁻¹. Find its velocity at t = 2. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 1500 × 12 = 2500v, v = 7.2 m s⁻¹ (2). Impulse on second car 1000 × 7.2 = 7200 N s (2).
2. 12 = 3v + 4.5, v = 2.5 m s⁻¹ (2). e = (4.5 − 2.5)/4 = 0.5 (3).
3. Speeds √(2g × 3.6) = 8.4 and √(2g × 1.6) = 5.6 (2). e = 5.6/8.4 = ⅔ (1).
4. Parallel 10 cos 60° = 5; perpendicular 10 sin 60° = 8.66 (1). After: perpendicular = 5 tan 45° = 5 (1). e = 5/8.66 = 0.577 (1). KE ratio after/before = (25 + 25)/100 = ½ (2). Fraction lost ½ (1).
5. I = ∫₀² (2t i + 3j) dt = 4i + 6j (2). 0.5(v − (i − j)) = 4i + 6j (1). v = 9i + 11j m s⁻¹ (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/47-centres-of-mass.md -->

## Centres of mass

This file covers the centre of mass of systems of particles in one and two dimensions, using symmetry and standard results, composite bodies and laminas (including removing parts), centres of mass by integration for laminas and solids of revolution, and bodies suspended freely or resting on a plane.

### Overview

Moments are in A-level Maths ([../maths/29-moments.md](../maths/29-moments.md)). Integration for volumes is in [12-further-calculus-volumes-improper-integrals-and-mean-value.md](12-further-calculus-volumes-improper-integrals-and-mean-value.md). Equilibrium, toppling and sliding are in [48-rigid-bodies-forces-and-equilibrium.md](48-rigid-bodies-forces-and-equilibrium.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA ME1 to ME5; Edexcel FM2 2.2, 3.1; OCR 6.04a to 6.04d; MEI MG1 to MG10.
- Standard results (rods, triangles, arcs, sectors, hemispheres, cones) are in each board's formula book; use them unless asked to derive.

### ME1 Systems of particles

*Refs: AQA ME1 · Edexcel FM2 2.2 · OCR 6.04a, 6.04b · MEI MG1. Level: AS & A (MEI).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
\bar x = \frac{\sum m_ix_i}{\sum m_i},\qquad \bar y = \frac{\sum m_iy_i}{\sum m_i}
```

- In vector form, r̄ = Σmᵢrᵢ/Σmᵢ.

#### Worked example

Particles of mass 3, 2 and 5 kg are at (2, 1), (−1, 4) and (5, −2). Find the centre of mass.

1. x̄ = (6 − 2 + 25)/10 = 2.9. ȳ = (3 + 8 − 10)/10 = 0.1.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing by the number of particles instead of the total mass.

### ME2 Composite bodies

*Refs: AQA ME2 · Edexcel FM2 2.2, 3.1 · OCR 6.04c · MEI MG2, MG3, MG4. Level: AS & A (MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- Use symmetry where possible.
- For a uniform lamina, mass is proportional to area. Treat each part as a particle at its own centre of mass.
- To remove a part, give it negative mass (area).
- Standard results: uniform triangle at the mean of the vertices; semicircular lamina 4r/(3π) from the diameter; solid hemisphere 3r/8; solid cone h/4 from the base.

#### Worked example

A uniform L-shaped lamina is made from a 6 × 2 rectangle (from (0, 0) to (6, 2)) and a 2 × 4 rectangle on top of its left end (from (0, 2) to (2, 6)). Find its centre of mass.

1. Parts: area 12 at (3, 1); area 8 at (1, 4).
2. x̄ = (36 + 8)/20 = 2.2. ȳ = (12 + 32)/20 = 2.2.

A circle of radius 1 centred at (4, 3) is cut from a uniform rectangular plate from (0, 0) to (8, 6). Find the centre of mass.

1. Plate area 48 at (4, 3); hole area π at (4, 3). The hole is at the centre, so the centre of mass stays at (4, 3).
2. If instead the hole is centred at (6, 3): x̄ = (48 × 4 − π × 6)/(48 − π) = 3.860.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding the removed part instead of subtracting it.

### ME3 Centres of mass by integration

*Refs: AQA ME3, ME4 · Edexcel FM2 2.2, 3.1 · OCR 6.04d · MEI MG6, MG7, MG8, MG9. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

For a uniform lamina under y = f(x) from x = a to b:

```latex
\bar x = \frac{\int_a^b xy\,dx}{\int_a^b y\,dx},\qquad \bar y = \frac{\int_a^b \tfrac12y^2\,dx}{\int_a^b y\,dx}
```

For a uniform solid of revolution about the x-axis:

```latex
\bar x = \frac{\int_a^b \pi xy^2\,dx}{\int_a^b \pi y^2\,dx}
```

#### Explained

Each vertical strip of width δx has area yδx and its own centre at (x, y/2). Summing moments gives the formulae. For solids, each disc has volume πy²δx at distance x.

#### Worked example

Find the centre of mass of the uniform lamina under y = x² from x = 0 to 2.

1. Area ∫₀² x² dx = 8/3.
2. x̄ = ∫₀² x³ dx ÷ 8/3 = 4 × 3/8 = 1.5.
3. ȳ = ½∫₀² x⁴ dx ÷ 8/3 = (16/5)(3/8) = 1.2.

The region under y = √x from x = 0 to 4 is rotated about the x-axis. Find the centre of mass of the solid.

1. Volume π∫₀⁴ x dx = 8π.
2. x̄ = π∫₀⁴ x² dx/8π = (64/3)/8 = 8/3.

Prove that a uniform solid hemisphere of radius r has its centre of mass 3r/8 from the plane face.

1. Rotate y² = r² − x², 0 ≤ x ≤ r. Volume π∫(r² − x²) dx = 2πr³/3.
2. π∫x(r² − x²) dx = π(r⁴/2 − r⁴/4) = πr⁴/4.
3. x̄ = (πr⁴/4)/(2πr³/3) = 3r/8.

#### Common mistakes

- Using y instead of y/2 for the lamina's ȳ.

### ME5 Suspension and resting on a plane

*Refs: AQA ME5 · OCR 6.04e · MEI MG5, MG10. Level: AS & A (MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- A body suspended freely from a point hangs with its centre of mass vertically below that point.
- A body on an inclined plane is on the point of toppling when the centre of mass is vertically above the lowest edge of contact.

#### Worked example

The L-shaped lamina above is suspended from the origin. Find the angle between the side along the x-axis and the vertical.

1. The line from (0, 0) to G(2.2, 2.2) is vertical.
2. It makes 45° with the x-axis, so the x-axis side is at 45° to the vertical.

A uniform cuboid 0.4 m wide and 1.2 m tall stands on a rough slope (friction prevents sliding). Find the angle at which it topples.

1. Topples when tan α = (half-width)/(height of G) = 0.2/0.6, α = 18.4°.

### Check questions

1. Masses of 1, 2, 3 and 4 kg are at the corners (0, 0), (4, 0), (4, 3), (0, 3) of a light rectangle. Find the centre of mass. [3 marks]
2. A uniform lamina is a square of side 6 with a square of side 2 removed from one corner. Find the distance of the centre of mass from each of the two full sides that meet at the corner opposite the removed square. [5 marks]
3. Find the centre of mass of the uniform lamina bounded by y = 4 − x², the x-axis and the y-axis (x ≥ 0). [6 marks]
4. Find the centre of mass of the uniform solid formed by rotating y = x, 0 ≤ x ≤ 3, about the x-axis (a cone). [4 marks]
5. State the distance of the centre of mass of a uniform solid hemisphere of radius 0.3 m from the centre of its plane face. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Total 10 (1). x̄ = (8 + 12)/10 = 2 (1). ȳ = (9 + 12)/10 = 2.1 (1).
2. Take the full square with corner at origin (area 36, centre (3, 3)) and remove the 2 × 2 square at the far corner (area 4, centre (5, 5)) (2). x̄ = (108 − 20)/32 = 2.75 (2). By symmetry ȳ = 2.75: distance 2.75 from each side through the origin (1).
3. Area ∫₀² (4 − x²) dx = 16/3 (1). ∫x(4 − x²) dx = 4 (1), x̄ = 3/4 (1). ½∫(4 − x²)² dx = ½(32 − 64/3 + 32/5) = 128/15 (2), ȳ = 1.6 (1).
4. Volume π∫x² dx = 9π (1). π∫x³ dx = 81π/4 (1). x̄ = 9/4 (from the vertex), so 3/4 from the base, which is h/4 (2).
5. 3r/8 = 0.1125 m (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/48-rigid-bodies-forces-and-equilibrium.md -->

## Forces, moments and equilibrium of rigid bodies

This file covers forces on particles (contact forces, friction, resolving, the triangle of forces) as MEI revisits them, moments and couples, the conditions for a rigid body to be in equilibrium, problems with ladders, hinged rods and strings, and deciding whether a body will slide or topple.

### Overview

Much of the particle work (marked † in the MEI specification) is shared with A-level Maths: see [../maths/27-forces-and-newtons-laws.md](../maths/27-forces-and-newtons-laws.md), [../maths/28-friction.md](../maths/28-friction.md) and [../maths/29-moments.md](../maths/29-moments.md). Centres of mass are in [47-centres-of-mass.md](47-centres-of-mass.md). Take g = 9.8 m s⁻².

#### Board differences

- AQA ME5, ME6 (sliding, toppling, suspension; forces on rigid bodies, moments and couples).
- Edexcel FM2 2.1 (moments), 3.2 (equilibrium of rigid bodies), 3.3 (toppling and sliding).
- OCR 6.04e (equilibrium of rigid bodies, including toppling).
- MEI Md1 to Md16 (Mechanics Major and Minor): Md1 to Md10 are particle forces and friction, including the result that a body on a rough plane is about to slip when tan α = μ (Md4) and closed polygons of forces (Md9); Md11 to Md16 are rigid bodies.

### Forces on particles

*Refs: MEI Md1, Md2, Md3, Md4, Md5, Md6, Md7, Md8, Md9, Md10. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The contact force between rough surfaces has a normal component R and a frictional component F ≤ μR, with F = μR when slipping or about to slip.
- Resolve in two perpendicular directions; in equilibrium each resolved sum is zero. Three forces in equilibrium form a closed triangle (use the sine or cosine rule).
- (Md4) A body on a rough plane inclined at α, with no other forces, is about to slip when tan α = μ.

#### Worked example

Show that a block on a rough plane is on the point of sliding when tan α = μ.

1. Perpendicular: R = mg cos α. Along: F = mg sin α.
2. Limiting: mg sin α = μmg cos α, so tan α = μ.

#### Common mistakes

- Assuming F = μR when the body is not about to slip.

### ME6 Moments, couples and rigid-body equilibrium

*Refs: AQA ME6 · Edexcel FM2 2.1, 3.2 · OCR 6.04e · MEI Md11, Md12, Md13, Md14, Md15. Level: AS & A (MEI); A-level (others).*

#### What you need to know

- Moment of F about a point = F × perpendicular distance from the point to the line of action.
- A couple is a pair of equal, opposite, parallel forces not in line. It has a turning effect (moment Fd, the same about every point) but no resultant force.
- A rigid body is in equilibrium if the resultant force is zero and the total moment about any point is zero.
- Take moments about a point where unknown forces act, to remove them from the equation.

#### Worked example

A uniform ladder of length 4 m and mass 20 kg rests against a smooth wall at 60° to rough horizontal ground. Find the least μ for equilibrium.

1. Forces: weight 20g at the midpoint, wall reaction S (horizontal), ground R and friction F.
2. Vertical: R = 20g. Horizontal: F = S.
3. Moments about the foot: S × 4 sin 60° = 20g × 2 cos 60°, so S = 10g cot 60° = 56.6 N.
4. F ≤ μR: μ ≥ 56.6/196 = 0.289.

A uniform horizontal rod of length 2 m and weight 50 N is hinged to a wall at A and held by a string from its end B to a point on the wall above A, at 30° to the rod. Find the tension and the hinge force.

1. Moments about A: T sin 30° × 2 = 50 × 1, T = 50 N.
2. Horizontal: H = T cos 30° = 43.3 N. Vertical: V = 50 − 25 = 25 N.
3. Hinge force √(43.3² + 25²) = 50 N, at 30° above the horizontal.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the length along the rod instead of the perpendicular distance.
- Forgetting the hinge reaction can act in any direction (two components).

### ME5 Sliding or toppling

*Refs: AQA ME5 · Edexcel FM2 3.3 · MEI Md16. Level: AS & A (MEI); A-level (others).*

#### What you need to know

- On the point of toppling, the normal reaction acts at the edge about which the body would tip.
- Compare the force (or angle) needed to cause sliding with that needed for toppling; the smaller one happens first.

#### Worked example

A uniform cube of side 0.4 m and weight 100 N on rough ground (μ = 0.6) is pushed by a horizontal force P at height 0.3 m. Does it slide or topple first as P increases?

1. Sliding: P = μR = 60 N.
2. Toppling about the front edge: P × 0.3 = 100 × 0.2, P = 66.7 N.
3. 60 < 66.7: it slides first.

#### Common mistakes

- Taking moments about the centre instead of the toppling edge.

### Check questions

1. A uniform beam AB of length 6 m and weight 200 N rests on supports at C and D, 1 m from each end. A 300 N weight is placed 0.5 m from A. Find the support forces. [4 marks]
2. A uniform ladder of weight W and length 2a rests against a smooth wall with its foot on rough ground (μ = 0.4). Find the smallest angle with the ground for equilibrium. [5 marks]
3. A couple and a force act on a lamina. Explain why the moment of a couple is the same about every point. [2 marks]
4. A uniform cuboid 0.5 m wide and 1 m tall, weight 80 N, is on rough ground (μ = 0.3). A horizontal force P acts at the top. Find whether it slides or topples first. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Supports at 1 m and 5 m from A. Moments about C: D × 4 = 200 × 2 + 300 × (−0.5) (2), D = 62.5 N (1). C = 500 − 62.5 = 437.5 N (1).
2. R = W, F = S (1). Moments about the foot: S × 2a sin θ = Wa cos θ (2), S = (W/2) cot θ (1). ½ cot θ ≤ 0.4 gives tan θ ≥ 1.25, θ ≥ 51.3° (1).
3. Forces F at distance d apart: about any point, moments Fx and F(d − x) or F(x + d) − Fx combine to Fd (2).
4. Slide: P = 0.3 × 80 = 24 N (1). Topple about the far bottom edge: P × 1 = 80 × 0.25, P = 20 N (2). 20 < 24: it topples first (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/46-circular-motion.md -->

## Circular motion

This file covers angular speed, the relationships v = rω and a = rω² = v²/r, motion in a horizontal circle (conical pendulums, banked tracks, cars on bends), vectors in circular motion, and motion in a vertical circle, including conditions for completing the circle and for leaving a surface.

### Overview

Newton's laws and friction are in A-level Maths ([../maths/27-forces-and-newtons-laws.md](../maths/27-forces-and-newtons-laws.md), [../maths/28-friction.md](../maths/28-friction.md)). Energy is in [43-work-energy-and-power.md](43-work-energy-and-power.md). Take g = 9.8 m s⁻².

#### Board differences

- AQA MD1 to MD6 (including conical pendulums with one or two strings, and position, velocity and acceleration as vectors).
- Edexcel FM2 1.1, 1.2.
- OCR 6.05a to 6.05f.
- MEI Mr1 to Mr8, including tangential acceleration (Mr6) and when a particle leaves a circular path (Mr8).

### MD1 Angular speed and acceleration towards the centre

*Refs: AQA MD1, MD2, MD3, MD4 · Edexcel FM2 1.1 · OCR 6.05a, 6.05b · MEI Mr1, Mr3. Level: AS & A (OCR); A-level (MEI, Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- Angular speed ω in rad s⁻¹; convert from revolutions per minute by × 2π/60.
- v = rω. Acceleration towards the centre a = rω² = v²/r, even at constant speed.
- (AQA MD4) With r = r cos ωt i + r sin ωt j: v = ṙ is tangential with magnitude rω, and a = r̈ = −ω²r points to the centre.

#### Worked example

A point on a wheel of radius 0.5 m turns at 45 rev min⁻¹. Find ω, v and a.

1. ω = 45 × 2π/60 = 4.71 rad s⁻¹. v = 2.36 m s⁻¹. a = 0.5 × 4.71² = 11.1 m s⁻².

#### Common mistakes

- Using degrees or revolutions in v = rω.

### MD5 Horizontal circles

*Refs: AQA MD5 · Edexcel FM2 1.2 · OCR 6.05c, 6.05d · MEI Mr2, Mr4. Level: AS & A (OCR).*

#### What you need to know

- Resolve vertically (forces balance) and horizontally (resultant = mrω² or mv²/r towards the centre).
- Conical pendulum: string at angle θ to the vertical, radius r = l sin θ.
- Banked track at angle α with no friction: v² = rg tan α. With friction, friction acts down the slope at high speeds and up it at low speeds.

#### Worked example

A conical pendulum: mass 0.2 kg, string 0.5 m at 30° to the vertical. Find the tension and ω.

1. Vertical: T cos 30° = 0.2 × 9.8, T = 2.26 N.
2. Horizontal: T sin 30° = 0.2 × 0.25 × ω², ω = 4.76 rad s⁻¹.

Find the speed at which a car can go round a bend of radius 50 m, banked at 20°, with no sideways friction.

1. R cos 20° = mg, R sin 20° = mv²/50, so v² = 50g tan 20°, v = 13.4 m s⁻¹.

Find the greatest speed on a flat bend of radius 40 m with μ = 0.6.

1. μmg = mv²/40, v = √(0.6 × 9.8 × 40) = 15.3 m s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Resolving along the slope instead of horizontally and vertically (the acceleration is horizontal).

### MD6 Vertical circles

*Refs: AQA MD6 · Edexcel FM2 1.2 · OCR 6.05e, 6.05f · MEI Mr5, Mr6, Mr7, Mr8. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Speed varies: use conservation of energy between two points.
- Radial equation: the resultant force towards the centre = mv²/r. At the top: T + mg = mv²/r; at the bottom: T − mg = mv²/r; at angle θ from the bottom: T − mg cos θ = mv²/r.
- Tangential acceleration (MEI Mr6) = g sin θ component along the path.
- A particle on a string completes the circle if T ≥ 0 at the top: v_top² ≥ gr, so u_bottom² ≥ 5gr. A bead on a wire or a particle on a rod only needs v ≥ 0 at the top: u² ≥ 4gr (just over).
- A particle on the outside of a smooth sphere leaves the surface when the reaction becomes 0.

#### Worked example

A 0.3 kg particle on a string of length 0.8 m moves in a vertical circle with speed 8 m s⁻¹ at the bottom. Find the speed and tension at the top, and the tension at the bottom.

1. Energy: v² = 64 − 2 × 9.8 × 1.6 = 32.64, v = 5.71 m s⁻¹.
2. Top: T + 0.3 × 9.8 = 0.3 × 32.64/0.8, T = 9.30 N.
3. Bottom: T − 2.94 = 0.3 × 64/0.8, T = 26.9 N.

Find the least speed at the bottom for the particle to complete the circle.

1. u² ≥ 5gr = 39.2, u ≥ 6.26 m s⁻¹.

A particle slides from rest at the top of a smooth sphere of radius r. Find where it leaves the surface.

1. At angle θ from the top: v² = 2gr(1 − cos θ).
2. Radial: mg cos θ − R = mv²/r. R = 0 gives g cos θ = 2g(1 − cos θ), cos θ = ⅔, θ = 48.2°.

#### Common mistakes

- Using u² ≥ 4gr for a string (that is the rod/bead condition).
- Getting the direction of the weight component wrong in the radial equation.

### Check questions

1. A particle on a smooth table is attached by a string of length 0.6 m to a fixed point and moves in a horizontal circle at 2 rev s⁻¹. Its mass is 0.4 kg. Find the tension. [3 marks]
2. A particle of mass 0.5 kg moves in a horizontal circle on the inside of a smooth hemispherical bowl of radius 0.4 m, at a depth of 0.2 m below the rim. Find its speed. [5 marks]
3. A string of length 0.8 m: the particle has speed 5 m s⁻¹ at the bottom. Find the angle above the horizontal at which the string goes slack. [5 marks]
4. A car of mass 900 kg travels at 25 m s⁻¹ round a bend of radius 80 m banked at 15°. Find the friction force and its direction. [6 marks]
5. A particle on a light rod of length 1 m moves in a vertical circle. Find the least speed at the bottom for it to reach the top. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. ω = 4π (1). T = 0.4 × 0.6 × (4π)² (1) = 37.9 N (1).
2. Radius of circle √(0.4² − 0.2²) = 0.3464 m (1). Reaction at angle with tan(angle to vertical) = 0.3464/0.2 (1). R cos α = mg, R sin α = mv²/r (1). v² = rg tan α = 0.3464 × 9.8 × 1.732 = 5.88 (1). v = 2.42 m s⁻¹ (1).
3. At angle a above horizontal, v² = 25 − 2g × 0.8(1 + sin a) (2). T = 0 gives v² = 0.8g sin a (1). 25 − 15.68 = 23.52 sin a (1), a = 23.3° (1).
4. Horizontal: R sin 15° + F cos 15° = 900 × 625/80 = 7031 (2). Vertical: R cos 15° − F sin 15° = 8820 (2). Solve: F = 7031 cos 15° − 8820 sin 15° = 4509 N (1), down the slope (1).
5. u² = 4gr = 39.2, u = 6.26 m s⁻¹ (just over) (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/49-variable-forces-kinematics-and-shm.md -->

## Variable forces, vector kinematics and simple harmonic motion

This file covers motion in a straight line under a force that depends on time, displacement or velocity (using a = dv/dt and a = v dv/dx), vector kinematics with calculus, the Cartesian path and range of a projectile (including on an inclined plane), and simple harmonic motion as a mechanics model.

### Overview

Kinematics with calculus and projectiles are in A-level Maths ([../maths/25-kinematics.md](../maths/25-kinematics.md), [../maths/26-projectiles.md](../maths/26-projectiles.md)). Solving the differential equations is in [16-differential-equations-first-order.md](16-differential-equations-first-order.md) and [17-differential-equations-second-order-and-oscillations.md](17-differential-equations-second-order-and-oscillations.md); Hooke's law in [44-elastic-strings-and-springs.md](44-elastic-strings-and-springs.md). Take g = 9.8 m s⁻².

#### Board differences

- Edexcel FM2 4.1 (Newton's laws with variable forces), 4.2 (simple harmonic motion, including proof that motion is SHM and the use of x = a sin ωt, v² = ω²(a² − x²)), 5.1 (kinematics with variable acceleration).
- OCR 6.06a: linear motion under a variable force in one dimension, using a = dv/dt or a = v dv/dx.
- MEI Mk1, Mk2, Mv1 to Mv12: vector kinematics, projectile paths and ranges (including maximum range), variable forces, and SHM.
- AQA's SHM and damping are in its compulsory core (I7 to I11, file 17).

### Forces depending on time, displacement or velocity

*Refs: Edexcel FM2 4.1, 5.1 · OCR 6.06a · MEI Mk1, Mk2, Mv8, Mv9, Mv10, Mv11. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- F = ma with a = dv/dt (when F depends on t or v) or a = v dv/dx (when F depends on x, or when you want v in terms of x).
- Separate variables and use the initial conditions.

#### Explained

Choose the form of a to match what the force depends on and what the question asks for. If the force depends on v and you want a distance, use v dv/dx so that dx appears.

#### Worked example

A particle of mass 2 kg moves along the x-axis under a force 6x N (directed away from O). It starts from rest at x = 1. Find its speed at x = 3.

1. 2v dv/dx = 6x, so ∫v dv = ∫3x dx, v²/2 = 3x²/2 + c.
2. v = 0 at x = 1: c = −3/2. v² = 3(x² − 1).
3. At x = 3: v² = 24, v = 4.90 m s⁻¹.

A 0.5 kg particle moving at 10 m s⁻¹ is slowed by a resistance 2v N. Find v in terms of t, and the total distance it travels.

1. 0.5 dv/dt = −2v, so v = 10e^{−4t}.
2. 0.5v dv/dx = −2v, dv/dx = −4, v = 10 − 4x.
3. v → 0 as x → 2.5, so the total distance is 2.5 m (never quite reached in finite time).

#### Common mistakes

- Using constant-acceleration formulae when the force varies.

### Vector kinematics and projectile paths

*Refs: MEI Mv1, Mv2, Mv3, Mv4, Mv5, Mv6, Mv7. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- v = dr/dt, a = dv/dt; integrate the other way with initial conditions.
- Projectile from the origin at speed u, angle θ: eliminate t to get the path

```latex
y = x\tan\theta - \frac{gx^2}{2u^2\cos^2\theta} = x\tan\theta - \frac{gx^2}{2u^2}(1 + \tan^2\theta)
```

- Range on horizontal ground u² sin 2θ/g, greatest (u²/g) at θ = 45°.
- On a plane inclined at α, use axes along and perpendicular to the plane, or intersect the path with y = x tan α.

#### Worked example

r = t³ i + (4t − t²) j. Find the velocity and acceleration at t = 2.

1. v = 3t² i + (4 − 2t) j = 12i at t = 2.
2. a = 6t i − 2j = 12i − 2j.

A ball is projected at 20 m s⁻¹ at 45° to the horizontal up a plane inclined at 15°. Find the range up the plane.

1. Path: y = x − 9.8x²/400 (since 2u²cos²45° = 400).
2. Plane: y = x tan 15° = 0.2679x. Intersect: x − 0.0245x² = 0.2679x, x = 29.88 m.
3. Range along the plane = 29.88/cos 15° = 30.9 m.

A target at (40, 10) is to be hit with speed 25 m s⁻¹. Find the possible angles of projection.

1. 10 = 40T − 9.8 × 1600(1 + T²)/1250, with T = tan θ: 12.544T² − 40T + 22.544 = 0.
2. T = 2.457 or 0.731, θ = 67.9° or 36.2°.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting cos²θ = 1/(1 + tan²θ) when solving for the angle.

### Simple harmonic motion

*Refs: Edexcel FM2 4.2 · MEI Mv12. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- SHM: ẍ = −ω²x, x measured from the centre of oscillation.
- x = a sin(ωt + ε) (or a cos ωt from an extreme point); v² = ω²(a² − x²); maximum speed aω at the centre; maximum acceleration aω² at the extremes; period 2π/ω.
- To prove motion is SHM, use Newton's second law with x from the equilibrium position and show ẍ = −ω²x.

#### Worked example

A particle moves with SHM of amplitude 0.2 m and period π s. Find its speed when 0.1 m from the centre.

1. ω = 2. v² = 4(0.04 − 0.01) = 0.12, v = 0.346 m s⁻¹.

A particle of mass m hangs on an elastic string (natural length l, modulus λ). Show that small vertical oscillations are SHM and find the period.

1. Equilibrium extension e: mg = λe/l.
2. At x below equilibrium (string taut): mẍ = mg − λ(e + x)/l = −λx/l.
3. ẍ = −(λ/ml)x: SHM with period 2π√(ml/λ).

Find the time for the particle in the first example to move from the centre to 0.1 m.

1. x = 0.2 sin 2t. 0.1 = 0.2 sin 2t, t = π/12 = 0.262 s.

#### Common mistakes

- Measuring x from the natural length rather than from equilibrium.

### Check questions

1. A particle of mass 3 kg moves along a line under a force (12 − 6t) N. At t = 0, v = 2 m s⁻¹. Find v at t = 3 and the greatest speed. [5 marks]
2. A particle of mass 1 kg moves away from O under a repulsive force 4/x² N. It starts from rest at x = 1. Find its speed at x = 4 and its limiting speed. [5 marks]
3. A projectile is fired from the origin at speed 30 m s⁻¹ at angle θ. Show that the greatest range on level ground is about 91.8 m. [3 marks]
4. A particle performs SHM with maximum speed 3 m s⁻¹ and maximum acceleration 12 m s⁻². Find the amplitude and period. [4 marks]
5. (MEI) r = 2cos t i + 3 sin t j. Show that the acceleration is always directed towards O, and find the Cartesian equation of the path. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 3 dv/dt = 12 − 6t, v = 2 + 4t − t² (2). t = 3: v = 5 m s⁻¹ (1). Greatest when 4 − 2t = 0, t = 2, v = 6 m s⁻¹ (2).
2. v dv/dx = 4/x² (1). v²/2 = −4/x + c, c = 4 (1). v² = 8(1 − 1/x) (1). x = 4: v = √6 = 2.45 m s⁻¹ (1). Limiting speed √8 = 2.83 m s⁻¹ (1).
3. Range u² sin 2θ/g (1), greatest when sin 2θ = 1 (1): 900/9.8 = 91.8 m (1).
4. aω = 3, aω² = 12, ω = 4 (2). a = 0.75 m (1). Period π/2 s (1).
5. a = −2cos t i − 3 sin t j = −r (2), so towards O. x²/4 + y²/9 = 1 (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/51-graph-theory.md -->

## Graph theory

This file covers the language of graphs, degree and the handshaking lemma, simple graphs, trees, complete and bipartite graphs, complements, adjacency matrices, digraphs, Eulerian and Hamiltonian graphs (including Ore's theorem), isomorphism, planarity (Euler's formula, Kuratowski's theorem, the planarity algorithm, thickness) and colouring arguments.

### Overview

Option content. Weighted graphs (networks) and their algorithms are in [52-network-algorithms.md](52-network-algorithms.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA DA1 to DA7: language, Eulerian, semi-Eulerian and Hamiltonian graphs, Euler's formula, Kuratowski's theorem, complete and bipartite graphs, adjacency matrices, complements, trees and isomorphism.
- Edexcel D1 1.4: the planarity algorithm (for graphs with a Hamiltonian cycle). Other graph language appears in the D1 glossary.
- OCR 7.02a to 7.02r: the widest coverage, including colouring arguments (7.02f), Ore's theorem (7.02i), digraphs (7.02k) and thickness (7.02o).
- MEI AN1, AN2: graph language and modelling problems with graphs.

### DA1 The language of graphs

*Refs: AQA DA1, DA5, DA6 · OCR 7.02a, 7.02b, 7.02c, 7.02d, 7.02e, 7.02k, 7.02q · MEI AN1, AN2. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- A graph has vertices (nodes) joined by edges (arcs). The degree (order) of a vertex is the number of edge ends at it; a loop counts 2.
- Handshaking lemma: the sum of degrees = 2 × number of edges, so the number of odd vertices is even.
- Walk: any sequence of edges; trail: no edge repeated; path: no vertex repeated; cycle: a closed path.
- Simple graph: no loops or multiple edges. Connected: a path between every pair of vertices. Subgraph; subdivision (edges split by new degree-2 vertices).
- Tree: connected with no cycles; a tree with n vertices has n − 1 edges. Spanning tree: a subgraph tree including every vertex.
- Kₙ: complete graph, every pair joined, n(n − 1)/2 edges. K_{m,n}: complete bipartite graph, mn edges.
- Complement of a simple graph G: same vertices, with an edge exactly where G has none.
- Adjacency matrix: entry (i, j) is the number of edges between i and j.
- Digraph: edges have directions; in-degree and out-degree.

#### Worked example

A simple graph has 6 vertices with degrees 1, 2, 2, 3, 3, x. Find the possible values of x.

1. The degree sum 11 + x must be even, so x is odd.
2. In a simple graph with 6 vertices, x ≤ 5. So x = 1, 3 or 5 (each can be confirmed by drawing a graph).

How many edges does the complement of K_{3,4} have?

1. K₇ has 21 edges; K_{3,4} has 12. Complement 9 edges (a K₃ and a K₄, disjoint).

#### Common mistakes

- Counting a loop as degree 1.

### DA2 Eulerian and Hamiltonian graphs

*Refs: AQA DA2 · OCR 7.02g, 7.02h, 7.02i. Level: AS & A (OCR 7.02h, 7.02i A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Eulerian: a connected graph with a closed trail using every edge exactly once; this happens if and only if every vertex has even degree.
- Semi-Eulerian: exactly two odd vertices; an open trail using every edge starts at one and ends at the other.
- Hamiltonian: has a cycle visiting every vertex exactly once. There is no simple test.
- Ore's theorem: a simple graph with n ≥ 3 vertices is Hamiltonian if deg(u) + deg(v) ≥ n for every pair of non-adjacent vertices u, v. (A sufficient condition, not necessary.)

#### Worked example

Which complete graphs Kₙ are Eulerian?

1. Every vertex has degree n − 1. Eulerian if n − 1 is even: n odd.

Show that K_{3,3} is Hamiltonian, and use Ore's theorem on it.

1. Cycle A1 B1 A2 B2 A3 B3 A1 visits all 6 vertices.
2. Non-adjacent pairs are in the same set, each with degree 3: 3 + 3 = 6 ≥ 6. Ore's theorem confirms it.

#### Common mistakes

- Thinking a Hamiltonian cycle must use every edge.

### DA7 Isomorphism

*Refs: AQA DA7 · OCR 7.02j. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Two graphs are isomorphic if there is a one-to-one matching of vertices that preserves adjacency.
- Isomorphic graphs have the same numbers of vertices and edges and the same degree sequence; these are necessary, not sufficient. Look also for cycles of a given length.

#### Worked example

Are the 6-cycle C₆ and two disjoint triangles isomorphic?

1. Both have 6 vertices, 6 edges and all degrees 2.
2. C₆ is connected; two triangles are not. Not isomorphic.

### DA3 Planarity

*Refs: AQA DA3, DA4 · Edexcel D1 1.4 · OCR 7.02f, 7.02l, 7.02m, 7.02n, 7.02o. Level: A-level only (OCR); option (AQA, Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- A planar graph can be drawn with no edges crossing. It divides the plane into regions (faces), including the outside region.
- Euler's formula for a connected planar graph: V + R = E + 2 (equivalently V − E + R = 2).
- For a simple connected planar graph with V ≥ 3: E ≤ 3V − 6 (each region has at least 3 edges). If there are no triangles: E ≤ 2V − 4.
- K₅ and K_{3,3} are not planar. Kuratowski's theorem: a graph is planar if and only if it has no subgraph that is a subdivision of K₅ or K_{3,3}.
- Planarity algorithm (Edexcel): draw a Hamiltonian cycle as a polygon, list the other edges, and place them inside or outside so that none cross (edges that would cross go on opposite sides). If this cannot be done, the graph is not planar.
- Thickness (OCR): the least number of planar graphs whose union is the graph. K₅ and K_{3,3} have thickness 2.
- (OCR 7.02f) Colouring arguments, for example showing a graph is bipartite (2-colourable) or not.

#### Worked example

Show that K₅ is not planar.

1. V = 5, E = 10. If planar and simple, E ≤ 3V − 6 = 9. But E = 10. Not planar.

Show that K_{3,3} is not planar.

1. V = 6, E = 9. Bipartite, so no triangles: E ≤ 2V − 4 = 8. But E = 9. Not planar.

A connected planar graph has 8 vertices and 12 edges. How many regions?

1. R = E + 2 − V = 6.

#### Common mistakes

- Using E ≤ 3V − 6 to prove a graph is planar (it only shows non-planarity).
- Forgetting the outside region.

### Check questions

1. A graph has degree sequence 2, 2, 3, 3, 4, 4. (a) How many edges? (b) Is it Eulerian, semi-Eulerian or neither? [3 marks]
2. Draw a simple graph with 5 vertices that is Eulerian but not Hamiltonian. [2 marks]
3. Find the number of edges of K₈, K_{4,5} and the complement of K_{4,5}. [3 marks]
4. Explain why a tree with 9 vertices has 8 edges and at least two vertices of degree 1. [3 marks]
5. A simple connected planar graph has 10 vertices and every region is a triangle (including the outside). Find the number of edges and regions. [3 marks]
6. Show that the graph obtained from K₆ by deleting 2 edges is not planar. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) Sum 18, 9 edges (1). (b) Two odd vertices (1): semi-Eulerian (1).
2. E.g. two triangles sharing one vertex ("bow tie"): all degrees even, but the shared vertex would be visited twice in any cycle through all vertices (2).
3. 28 (1). 20 (1). K₉ has 36 edges, so 16 (1).
4. A tree is connected with no cycles; adding vertices one at a time each adds one edge, giving n − 1 = 8 (2). Degree sum 16 over 9 vertices with each degree ≥ 1, and the longest path's end vertices have degree 1 (1).
5. 3R = 2E and V + R = E + 2 (1). E = 3V − 6 = 24 (1). R = 16 (1).
6. V = 6, E = 15 − 2 = 13 (1). 3V − 6 = 12 (1). 13 > 12, so not planar (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/52-network-algorithms.md -->

## Network algorithms: spanning trees, shortest paths, route inspection and the travelling salesperson

This file covers networks (weighted graphs), minimum spanning trees by Kruskal's and Prim's algorithms (including the matrix form of Prim), shortest paths by Dijkstra's and Floyd's algorithms, the route inspection (Chinese postman) problem, and upper and lower bounds for the travelling salesperson problem.

### Overview

Option content. Graph language and Eulerian graphs are in [51-graph-theory.md](51-graph-theory.md). The example network used below has vertices A to F and edges AB 7, AC 4, AD 9, BC 5, BE 6, CD 3, CE 8, DE 7, DF 6, EF 5 (total weight 60).

#### Board differences

- AQA DB1 to DB5: minimum spanning trees, route inspection, TSP bounds, and evaluating network models. AQA does not name Dijkstra's algorithm.
- Edexcel D1 2.1, 2.2 (Kruskal, Prim including on a distance matrix, Dijkstra, Floyd), 3.1, 3.2 (route inspection with up to four odd vertices; classical and practical TSP, nearest neighbour, lower bounds, tour improvement).
- OCR 7.02p, 7.02r, 7.04a to 7.04f (Stage 2 adds nearest neighbour, lower bounds and route inspection).
- MEI AN3 to AN7 (Kruskal, Prim, Dijkstra; their complexities).

### DB2 Minimum spanning trees

*Refs: AQA DB1, DB2 · Edexcel D1 2.1 · OCR 7.02p, 7.04a · MEI AN3, AN4, AN5, AN7. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A minimum spanning tree (minimum connector) connects all vertices with the least total weight.
- Kruskal: list edges in increasing weight; add each edge unless it forms a cycle; stop at n − 1 edges.
- Prim: start at any vertex; repeatedly add the least-weight edge joining a vertex in the tree to one outside.
- Prim on a distance matrix: cross out the chosen vertex's row, label its column, and look down the labelled columns for the smallest undeleted entry.
- (MEI AN7) Kruskal's, Prim's and Dijkstra's algorithms are quadratic (O(n²)) in the number of vertices for matrix implementations.

#### Worked example

Find a minimum spanning tree of the example network using Kruskal's algorithm.

1. Order: CD 3, AC 4, BC 5, EF 5, BE 6, DF 6, AB 7, DE 7, CE 8, AD 9.
2. Add CD, AC, BC, EF, BE. (DF and later edges are not needed once 5 edges are chosen.)
3. Total weight 3 + 4 + 5 + 5 + 6 = 23.

Prim's algorithm from A gives the order AC, CD, CB, BE, EF, the same tree.

#### Common mistakes

- In Kruskal, adding an edge that makes a cycle.
- In Prim, choosing the overall smallest edge rather than one connected to the tree.

### Shortest paths

*Refs: Edexcel D1 2.2 · OCR 7.04b · MEI AN6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Dijkstra: give the start vertex the permanent label 0. From the latest permanent vertex, update working values of its neighbours (keep the smallest). Make the smallest working value permanent. Repeat. Trace the route back using edges whose weight equals the difference in labels.
- Floyd (Edexcel): uses a distance table and a route table, updating every entry through each vertex in turn; it finds shortest paths between all pairs.

#### Worked example

Use Dijkstra's algorithm to find the shortest distances from A in the example network.

1. A 0. Working: B 7, C 4, D 9.
2. C 4 permanent. Update D: 4 + 3 = 7; E: 4 + 8 = 12; B stays 7.
3. B 7 and D 7 permanent (either order). From B: E min(12, 13) = 12. From D: F 13, E stays 12.
4. E 12, F 13 permanent.
5. Shortest A to F: 13, route A–C–D–F.

#### Common mistakes

- Making a vertex permanent before it has the smallest working value.

### DB3 Route inspection

*Refs: AQA DB3 · Edexcel D1 3.1 · OCR 7.04e. Level: A-level only (OCR); option (AQA, Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- To traverse every edge and return to the start: if all vertices are even, the length is the total weight.
- Otherwise pair up the odd vertices so that the total of the shortest paths between pairs is least, and repeat those paths.
- With 4 odd vertices there are 3 pairings. If the route may start and finish at different vertices, repeat only the shortest path between one pair of odd vertices and start and finish at the other two.

#### Worked example

Find the shortest closed route along every edge of the example network.

1. Degrees: A 3, B 3, C 4, D 4, E 4, F 2. Odd: A, B.
2. Shortest A to B is 7 (direct). Repeat AB.
3. Route length 60 + 7 = 67.

#### Common mistakes

- Repeating edges between odd vertices without finding the shortest path.

### DB4 The travelling salesperson problem

*Refs: AQA DB4, DB5 · Edexcel D1 3.2 · OCR 7.04c, 7.04d, 7.04f. Level: A-level only (OCR); option (AQA, Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- Classical problem: visit each vertex exactly once and return. Practical problem: visit each vertex at least once (replace the network by a complete table of shortest distances).
- Upper bound: the nearest neighbour algorithm gives a tour; the best upper bound is the smallest tour found. (Or double the MST and shortcut.)
- Lower bound: delete a vertex, find the MST of the rest, then add the two shortest edges from the deleted vertex. The best lower bound is the largest found.
- Optimal tour length lies between the best lower and upper bounds; if they are equal, the tour is optimal.

#### Worked example

Find upper and lower bounds for the practical TSP on the example network.

1. Nearest neighbour from A (using shortest distances): A–C 4, C–D 3, D–F 6, F–E 5, E–B 6, B–A 7. Total 31.
2. Lower bound deleting A: MST of B to F is CD 3, BC 5, EF 5, BE 6 = 19. Two shortest edges from A: 4 and 7. Lower bound 30.
3. 30 ≤ optimal ≤ 31. (Here the optimal tour is 31.)

#### Common mistakes

- Taking the smallest lower bound as the best one.

### Check questions

1. Use Prim's algorithm on this distance matrix, starting from P, and state the weight of the tree. P–Q 8, P–R 5, P–S 9, Q–R 4, Q–S 6, R–S 7. [4 marks]
2. In the example network, use Dijkstra to find the shortest route from B to F. [4 marks]
3. A network has odd vertices W, X, Y, Z with shortest distances WX 5, WY 8, WZ 6, XY 7, XZ 9, YZ 4 and total weight 120. Find the length of the shortest closed route inspection, and the shortest route if it may start and end at different vertices. [5 marks]
4. Explain why the nearest neighbour algorithm gives an upper bound and the deleted-vertex method a lower bound. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. P–R 5, R–Q 4, Q–S 6 (3). Weight 15 (1).
2. B 0; C 5, A 7, E 6 (1). C permanent 5: D 8 (1). E permanent 6: F 11 (1). Shortest B–E–F 11 (1).
3. Pairings: WX + YZ = 9, WY + XZ = 17, WZ + XY = 13 (2). Closed route 120 + 9 = 129 (1). Open route: repeat only the shortest pair distance, 4 (YZ), starting and finishing at W and X: 124 (2).
4. Nearest neighbour gives an actual tour, so the optimum is no longer (1). The deleted-vertex method: any tour minus a vertex is a spanning tree of the rest (weight ≥ MST) plus two edges at the vertex (weight ≥ the two shortest) (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/50-algorithms-sorting-packing-and-complexity.md -->

## Algorithms: sorting, packing and complexity

This file covers what an algorithm is, tracing algorithms given as flowcharts, pseudocode or words, bubble sort, shuttle sort and quick sort, binary search, bin packing (next fit, first fit, first fit decreasing, full bins), heuristics, and the order (complexity) of an algorithm.

### Overview

Option content for Edexcel D1, OCR Discrete and MEI Modelling with Algorithms. Graph and network algorithms are in [51-graph-theory.md](51-graph-theory.md) and [52-network-algorithms.md](52-network-algorithms.md). AQA's Discrete option does not include sorting or packing.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel D1 1.1 to 1.3: algorithms, bubble sort, quick sort (pivot = the middle item, or the one to the right of the middle for an even list), binary search, first fit, first fit decreasing, full bin packing, and orders of algorithms.
- OCR 7.03a to 7.03m: algorithms, efficiency and big-O, bubble and shuttle sorts (Stage 1), quick sort (Stage 2), next fit, first fit, first fit decreasing and further packing.
- MEI AA1 to AA11: algorithms (including proving correctness and heuristics), quick sort, counting comparisons and swaps, first fit and first fit decreasing.

### Algorithms and heuristics

*Refs: Edexcel D1 1.1 · OCR 7.03a, 7.03b, 7.03c · MEI AA1, AA2, AA3, AA4, AA5, AA6. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- An algorithm is a finite sequence of precise instructions that solves a problem; it has an input, an output and must stop.
- Trace an algorithm with a table of variable values, step by step.
- A heuristic algorithm gives a good solution quickly but not necessarily the optimal one (for example first fit, nearest neighbour).
- (MEI) Repair or adapt an algorithm; argue that an algorithm is correct.

#### Worked example

Trace: "Let A = 48, B = 18. While B ≠ 0: let R = remainder of A ÷ B, A = B, B = R. Output A."

| A | B | R |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 48 | 18 | 12 |
| 18 | 12 | 6 |
| 12 | 6 | 0 |
| 6 | 0 | |

Output 6: the algorithm finds the highest common factor (Euclid's algorithm).

#### Common mistakes

- Updating variables in the wrong order when tracing.

### Sorting and searching

*Refs: Edexcel D1 1.2 · OCR 7.03j, 7.03k · MEI AA7, AA8, AA9. Level: AS & A (quick sort A-level only on OCR).*

#### What you need to know

- **Bubble sort**: compare adjacent pairs from the start, swapping if out of order; each pass puts the next largest at the end. Stop after a pass with no swaps. At most n − 1 passes and n(n − 1)/2 comparisons.
- **Shuttle sort** (OCR): on pass k, take the (k + 1)th item and move it left past larger items.
- **Quick sort**: choose a pivot, split the list into items less than the pivot (kept in order) and items greater, then repeat on each sublist until every item has been a pivot.
- **Binary search** (Edexcel) on an ordered list: compare with the middle item (pivot ⌈(first + last)/2⌉), discard the half that cannot contain the target, repeat.

#### Worked example

Bubble sort 23, 8, 15, 42, 4, 16, 31 into ascending order.

1. Pass 1: 8, 15, 23, 4, 16, 31, 42.
2. Pass 2: 8, 15, 4, 16, 23, 31, 42.
3. Pass 3: 8, 4, 15, 16, 23, 31, 42.
4. Pass 4: 4, 8, 15, 16, 23, 31, 42.
5. Pass 5: no swaps, stop. Comparisons: 6 + 5 + 4 + 3 + 2 = 20.

Quick sort the same list (middle pivot).

1. Pivot 42: 23, 8, 15, 4, 16, 31 | 42.
2. Pivot 4: 4 | 23, 8, 15, 16, 31 | 42.
3. Pivot 15: 4 | 8 | 15 | 23, 16, 31 | 42.
4. Pivot 16: ... | 16 | 23, 31 | 42. Pivot 31: 23 | 31.
5. Sorted: 4, 8, 15, 16, 23, 31, 42.

Use binary search to find 35 in 3, 7, 12, 18, 21, 26, 30, 35, 41, 47, 52.

1. First 1, last 11: pivot 6th item 26 < 35, so first = 7.
2. Pivot ⌈(7 + 11)/2⌉ = 9th item 41 > 35, so last = 8.
3. Pivot ⌈7.5⌉ = 8th item 35: found in 3 comparisons.

#### Common mistakes

- Stopping bubble sort before a pass with no swaps.
- Reordering the items in a quick sort sublist.

### Bin packing

*Refs: Edexcel D1 1.2 · OCR 7.03l, 7.03m · MEI AA10, AA11. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Lower bound for the number of bins = ⌈total size ÷ bin capacity⌉.
- Next fit: put each item in the current bin if it fits, otherwise start a new bin (never go back).
- First fit: put each item in the first bin with room.
- First fit decreasing: sort into decreasing order first, then first fit.
- Full bin packing: look for combinations that fill bins exactly, then first fit the rest.
- These are heuristics: they may not give the optimal packing.

#### Worked example

Pack items 8, 7, 14, 9, 6, 9, 5, 15, 6, 7 into bins of capacity 20.

1. Total 86, lower bound ⌈4.3⌉ = 5.
2. Next fit: [8, 7], [14], [9, 6], [9, 5], [15], [6, 7]: 6 bins.
3. First fit: [8, 7, 5], [14, 6], [9, 9], [15], [6, 7]: 5 bins.
4. First fit decreasing (15, 14, 9, 9, 8, 7, 7, 6, 6, 5): [15, 5], [14, 6], [9, 9], [8, 7], [7, 6]: 5 bins, optimal since it meets the lower bound.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to round the lower bound up.

### Order of an algorithm

*Refs: Edexcel D1 1.3 · OCR 7.03d, 7.03e, 7.03f, 7.03g, 7.03h, 7.03i · MEI AA8. Level: AS & A (OCR 7.03h, 7.03i A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- The order describes how the run time grows with the size n of the problem. Bubble sort is quadratic O(n²) in the worst case; first fit decreasing is O(n²); checking every pair of n items is O(n²); a triple loop is O(n³).
- Hierarchy (OCR): O(log n), O(n), O(n log n), O(n²), O(n³), O(aⁿ), from fastest to slowest growing.
- If an O(n²) algorithm takes t for size n, it takes about t(m/n)² for size m.

#### Worked example

A bubble sort of 100 items takes 0.2 s. Estimate the time for 500 items.

1. Quadratic: 0.2 × (500/100)² = 5 s.

An O(n³) algorithm takes 4 s for n = 30. How long for n = 90?

1. 4 × 3³ = 108 s.

#### Common mistakes

- Scaling linearly for a quadratic algorithm.

### Check questions

1. Use a bubble sort to put 12, 5, 9, 3, 15, 7 into descending order, showing each pass and the number of comparisons and swaps. [5 marks]
2. Use quick sort (middle pivot) to put 47, 12, 33, 8, 25, 61, 19, 40 into ascending order. [4 marks]
3. Items of size 4, 9, 3, 7, 5, 8, 6, 2, 9, 3 are packed into bins of capacity 15. (a) Find a lower bound. (b) Use first fit decreasing. (c) Is your packing optimal? [6 marks]
4. A first fit decreasing algorithm takes 0.6 s for 200 items. Estimate the time for 800 items, stating an assumption. [2 marks]
5. Use binary search to look for 20 in 2, 5, 9, 14, 17, 23, 28, 31. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Pass 1: 12, 9, 5, 15, 7, 3; pass 2: 12, 9, 15, 7, 5, 3; pass 3: 12, 15, 9, 7, 5, 3; pass 4: 15, 12, 9, 7, 5, 3; pass 5: no swaps (3). Comparisons 5 + 4 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 15 (1); swaps 3 + 2 + 1 + 1 = 7 (1).
2. Pivot 25 (5th of 8): 12, 8, 19 | 25 | 47, 33, 61, 40 (1). Sublists sorted with further pivots (2). 8, 12, 19, 25, 33, 40, 47, 61 (1).
3. (a) Total 56, ⌈56/15⌉ = 4 (2). (b) 9, 9, 8, 7, 6, 5, 4, 3, 3, 2: [9, 6], [9, 5], [8, 7], [4, 3, 3, 2] (3). (c) 4 bins = lower bound, so optimal (1).
4. Quadratic: 0.6 × 4² = 9.6 s, assuming time is proportional to n² (2).
5. Pivot ⌈(1 + 8)/2⌉ = 5th, 17 < 20: first = 6 (1). Pivot 7th, 28 > 20: last = 6 (1). Pivot 6th, 23 ≠ 20; list exhausted, 20 not in the list (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/53-critical-path-analysis.md -->

## Critical path analysis

This file covers precedence tables and activity networks (on node and on arc, with dummies), forward and backward passes, earliest and latest times, critical activities and paths, float, Gantt (cascade) charts, resource histograms, resource levelling and scheduling with limited workers.

### Overview

Option content. Graph language is in [51-graph-theory.md](51-graph-theory.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA DE1 to DE6 uses activity-on-node networks, and includes refining models, Gantt charts, resource histograms and resource levelling.
- Edexcel D1 4.1 to 4.6 uses activity-on-arc networks with dummies, total float, a lower bound for the number of workers, resource histograms, and scheduling.
- OCR 7.05a to 7.05e: activity networks, forward and backward passes, float, cascade charts and resources.
- MEI AN8, AN9 uses activity-on-arc.

### DE1 Activity networks

*Refs: AQA DE1 · Edexcel D1 4.1, 4.2 · OCR 7.05a · MEI AN8. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A precedence table lists each activity, its duration and its immediate predecessors.
- Activity on node: each activity is a node; arrows show precedence; add start and end nodes.
- Activity on arc: each activity is an arc between event nodes. Dummies (dashed, zero duration) show dependencies that cannot otherwise be drawn, and make sure each activity is uniquely defined by its start and end events.

#### Worked example

| Activity | Duration | Preceded by |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A | 3 | – |
| B | 5 | – |
| C | 4 | A |
| D | 6 | A |
| E | 2 | B, C |
| F | 5 | D, E |
| G | 4 | E |

In activity on arc: number the events 0 to 5.

1. A goes 0 → 1 and B goes 0 → 2. C goes 1 → 2, so B and C both end at event 2, which starts E.
2. D goes 1 → 4. E goes 2 → 3, and G leaves event 3 (G depends on E only).
3. F needs both D and E, so draw a dummy from event 3 to event 4; F goes 4 → 5, and G ends at 5.

#### Common mistakes

- Two activities with the same start and end events (use a dummy).

### DE2 Earliest and latest times, critical path and float

*Refs: AQA DE2, DE3, DE4 · Edexcel D1 4.3 · OCR 7.05b, 7.05c, 7.05d · MEI AN9. Level: AS & A (OCR 7.05d A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Forward pass: earliest start = the largest earliest finish of the predecessors.
- Backward pass: latest finish = the smallest latest start of the successors; the project's latest finish = the minimum completion time.
- Critical activity: earliest start = latest start (zero float). A critical path is a path of critical activities from start to end.
- Total float = latest finish − duration − earliest start.

#### Worked example

For the table above, find the earliest and latest times, the critical activities and the floats.

| Activity | ES | EF | LS | LF | Float |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A | 0 | 3 | 0 | 3 | 0 |
| B | 0 | 5 | 2 | 7 | 2 |
| C | 3 | 7 | 3 | 7 | 0 |
| D | 3 | 9 | 3 | 9 | 0 |
| E | 7 | 9 | 7 | 9 | 0 |
| F | 9 | 14 | 9 | 14 | 0 |
| G | 9 | 13 | 10 | 14 | 1 |

1. Minimum completion time 14.
2. Critical activities A, C, D, E, F; critical paths A–C–E–F and A–D–F.
3. (AQA DE4) If D takes 7 instead of 6, the project takes 15; if B takes 6, nothing changes because B has 2 float.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the smallest earliest finish in the forward pass.

### DE5 Gantt charts, resources and scheduling

*Refs: AQA DE5, DE6 · Edexcel D1 4.4, 4.5, 4.6 · OCR 7.05e. Level: A-level only (OCR); option (AQA, Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- Gantt (cascade) chart: each activity as a bar from its earliest start, with its float shown; critical activities on one line.
- Resource histogram: number of workers needed at each time.
- Lower bound for workers (Edexcel) = ⌈sum of all durations ÷ critical path length⌉.
- Resource levelling: use float to move non-critical activities to smooth the histogram or keep within a worker limit, possibly extending the project.
- Scheduling: at each time, give an available worker the activity with the least latest finish (or least float) that can start.

#### Worked example

Assuming each activity needs one worker, find a lower bound for the number of workers to finish in 14 days.

1. Sum of durations 29. ⌈29/14⌉ = 3.

With one worker per activity, how many workers are needed at time 3.5 if everything starts as early as possible?

1. Running at 3.5: B (0 to 5), C (3 to 7), D (3 to 9). Three workers.

#### Common mistakes

- Rounding the worker lower bound down.

### Check questions

1. Draw an activity-on-node network for: P (2, –), Q (4, –), R (3, P), S (5, P, Q), T (2, R, S). Find the minimum completion time and the critical path. [5 marks]
2. For question 1, find the float of each activity. [3 marks]
3. A project has total activity time 47 and critical path length 15. Find a lower bound for the number of workers. [2 marks]
4. Explain why a dummy might be needed in an activity-on-arc network. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Network drawn correctly (2). ES: P 0, Q 0, R 2, S 4, T 9; completion 11 (2). Critical path Q–S–T (1).
2. LF: T 11, S 9, R 9, P 4, Q 4. Floats: P 2, Q 0, R 4, S 0, T 0 (3).
3. ⌈47/15⌉ = ⌈3.13⌉ = 4 (2).
4. To show a dependency that a single arc cannot (when activities share some but not all predecessors) (1), or to keep each activity uniquely identified by its events (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/54-linear-programming-and-simplex.md -->

## Linear programming and the simplex algorithm

This file covers formulating linear programming (LP) problems, graphical solution (objective line and vertex methods), integer solutions, slack variables, the simplex algorithm and interpreting tableaux, problems with ≥ constraints (two-stage simplex and big-M), and sensitivity of the solution to changes.

### Overview

Option content. Inequalities and regions are in A-level Maths ([../maths/02-algebra-indices-surds-quadratics-and-inequalities.md](../maths/02-algebra-indices-surds-quadratics-and-inequalities.md)). Game theory problems solved as LPs are in [56-game-theory.md](56-game-theory.md); transportation and allocation formulations in [57-transportation-and-allocation.md](57-transportation-and-allocation.md); network flow formulations in [55-network-flows.md](55-network-flows.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA DD1 to DD4: formulation, graphical solution, simplex for maximising and minimising, interpreting tableaux.
- Edexcel D1 5.1 to 5.4: formulation, graphical solution (including integer solutions), simplex for maximising, two-stage simplex and big-M for ≥ constraints.
- OCR 7.06a to 7.06f, 7.07a to 7.07f: formulation, graphical solution and sensitivity (7.06e), simplex and its geometric meaning.
- MEI AL1 to AL18: formulation (including network problems, AL6), integer LP, 3D visualisation, simplex, two-stage simplex, free variables (AL16) and interpreting software output (AL18).

### DD1 Formulating linear programmes

*Refs: AQA DD1 · Edexcel D1 5.1 · OCR 7.06a · MEI AL1, AL2, AL3, AL5, AL6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Define the decision variables (with units), write the objective function to maximise or minimise, and list the constraints, including non-negativity.
- Recognise when variables must be integers.

#### Worked example

A bakery makes x trays of bread (profit £4, 2 hours of oven time, 4 kg flour) and y trays of cakes (profit £5, 3 hours, 1 kg flour). It has 12 oven hours and 14 kg of flour a day. Formulate the LP.

1. Maximise P = 4x + 5y.
2. Subject to 2x + 3y ≤ 12, 4x + y ≤ 14, x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0.

#### Common mistakes

- Leaving out non-negativity, or not defining variables.

### DD2 Graphical solution

*Refs: AQA DD2 · Edexcel D1 5.2 · OCR 7.06c, 7.06d, 7.06e, 7.06f · MEI AL7, AL8, AL9, AL10, AL11. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Draw each constraint line and shade the unwanted side; the feasible region is left clear.
- The optimum is at a vertex: slide the objective line (ruler method) or evaluate the objective at each vertex.
- For integer solutions, check integer points near the optimal vertex.
- Sensitivity: changing a coefficient in the objective changes the gradient of the objective line and may move the optimum to a different vertex.

#### Worked example

Solve the bakery LP graphically.

1. Vertices: (0, 0), (3.5, 0), (3, 2) where the lines meet, (0, 4).
2. P: 0, 14, 22, 20. Maximum P = 22 at x = 3, y = 2.

Minimise C = 2x + 3y subject to x + y ≥ 5, x ≤ 4, y ≤ 4, x, y ≥ 0.

1. Vertices of the feasible region: (4, 1), (4, 4), (1, 4).
2. C: 11, 20, 14. Minimum C = 11 at (4, 1).

For what values of k does the bakery's optimum stay at (3, 2) if the profit on bread is £k?

1. The objective line kx + 5y must lie between the gradients of the constraints through (3, 2): −2/3 and −4.
2. −4 ≤ −k/5 ≤ −2/3 gives 10/3 ≤ k ≤ 20.

#### Common mistakes

- Shading the feasible region instead of the unwanted regions (follow your board's convention and say which).

### DD3 The simplex algorithm

*Refs: AQA DD3, DD4 · Edexcel D1 5.3 · OCR 7.06b, 7.07a, 7.07b, 7.07c, 7.07d, 7.07e, 7.07f · MEI AL4, AL12, AL13, AL18. Level: A-level only (OCR); option (others).*

#### What you need to know

- Add a slack variable to each ≤ constraint to make an equation.
- Initial tableau: rows for constraints, objective row P − 4x − 5y = 0.
- Pivot column: the most negative entry in the objective row. Pivot row: smallest non-negative ratio (value ÷ positive entry in the pivot column).
- Divide the pivot row by the pivot; subtract multiples of it from other rows to make the rest of the column zero.
- Stop when there are no negative entries in the objective row. Read basic variables from columns with a single 1; non-basic variables are 0.
- Each tableau corresponds to a vertex of the feasible region.
- To minimise C, maximise −C.

#### Worked example

Solve the bakery LP by simplex.

Initial tableau:

| Basic | x | y | s₁ | s₂ | Value |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| s₁ | 2 | 3 | 1 | 0 | 12 |
| s₂ | 4 | 1 | 0 | 1 | 14 |
| P | −4 | −5 | 0 | 0 | 0 |

1. Pivot column y; ratios 12/3 = 4, 14/1 = 14; pivot row s₁.
2. After the first iteration: y row (2/3, 1, 1/3, 0, 4); s₂ row (10/3, 0, −1/3, 1, 10); P row (−2/3, 0, 5/3, 0, 20).
3. Pivot column x; ratios 4 ÷ 2/3 = 6, 10 ÷ 10/3 = 3; pivot row s₂.
4. Second iteration: x row (1, 0, −1/10, 3/10, 3); y row (0, 1, 2/5, −1/5, 2); P row (0, 0, 8/5, 1/5, 22).
5. No negatives: optimal. x = 3, y = 2, P = 22, both slacks 0 (all oven time and flour used).

#### Common mistakes

- Choosing a negative ratio, or dividing by a negative pivot entry.

### Two-stage simplex and big-M

*Refs: Edexcel D1 5.4 · MEI AL14, AL15, AL16, AL17. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A ≥ constraint gets a surplus variable and an artificial variable: x + y − s + a = 5.
- Two-stage: first minimise the sum of artificial variables (maximise −Σa); when it reaches 0, drop the artificial columns and continue with the real objective.
- Big-M: include −Ma for each artificial variable in the objective, M a large number, and run simplex once.
- An equality constraint can be written as two inequalities (≤ and ≥). A variable allowed to be negative can be replaced by the difference of two non-negative variables (MEI AL16).

#### Explained

Artificial variables give an obvious starting basic feasible solution even though the origin is not feasible. Stage 1 finds a feasible vertex; stage 2 optimises from it.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to express the stage 1 objective in terms of non-basic variables before starting.

### Check questions

1. A firm makes x chairs and y tables. A chair takes 2 hours to make and 1 hour to finish; a table 3 hours and 2 hours. 60 making hours and 35 finishing hours are available. Profit is £20 per chair and £35 per table. Formulate the LP and solve it graphically. [7 marks]
2. Use the simplex algorithm to maximise P = 2x + 3y subject to x + 2y ≤ 8, 3x + y ≤ 9, x, y ≥ 0. [8 marks]
3. A final tableau has P row (0, 0, 1.5, 0.5, 0, 42). Explain how you know it is optimal and what the zero slack variables mean. [2 marks]
4. Show how to set up the first tableau of a two-stage simplex for: maximise P = x + 2y subject to x + y ≥ 3, x + 2y ≤ 10. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Maximise P = 20x + 35y (1); 2x + 3y ≤ 60, x + 2y ≤ 35, x, y ≥ 0 (2). Vertices (0, 0), (30, 0), (15, 10), (0, 17.5) (2). P: 0, 600, 650, 612.5 (1). Maximum £650 with 15 chairs and 10 tables (1).
2. Tableau with slacks (1). Pivot y column, ratios 4 and 9, pivot row 1 (1). Row ops correct (2). Next pivot x column (1). Final x = 2, y = 3 (2), P = 13 (1).
3. No negative entries in the objective row (1). Those constraints are fully used (binding) (1).
4. x + y − s₁ + a₁ = 3, x + 2y + s₂ = 10 (2). Stage 1 objective: maximise I = −a₁, written as I − x − y + s₁ = −3 after substituting a₁ (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/56-game-theory.md -->

## Game theory

This file covers two-person zero-sum games, pay-off matrices, play-safe strategies and the value of a game, stable solutions (saddle points), dominance, optimal mixed strategies by the graphical method, converting larger games to linear programmes, and (OCR) Nash equilibria in non-zero-sum games.

### Overview

Option content. Linear programming and simplex are in [54-linear-programming-and-simplex.md](54-linear-programming-and-simplex.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA DF1 to DF6; Edexcel D2 5.1 to 5.5; OCR 7.08a to 7.08f. MEI does not include game theory.
- OCR 7.08d (Stage 2) includes Nash equilibria for games that are not zero-sum.

### DF1 Pay-off matrices, play-safe strategies and stability

*Refs: AQA DF1, DF2, DF3 · Edexcel D2 5.1, 5.2 · OCR 7.08a, 7.08c. Level: AS & A (OCR).*

#### What you need to know

- In a two-person zero-sum game, the pay-off matrix shows the row player's gains (the column player's losses).
- Row player's play-safe strategy: the row with the largest minimum (maximin). Column player's: the column with the smallest maximum (minimax).
- If maximin = minimax, the game has a stable solution (saddle point) and that number is the value of the game. Neither player can gain by changing strategy alone.

#### Worked example

| | B1 | B2 | B3 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A1 | 4 | 2 | 5 |
| A2 | 3 | 1 | 0 |
| A3 | 6 | 2 | 3 |

1. Row minima: 2, 0, 2. Maximin 2 (A1 or A3).
2. Column maxima: 6, 2, 5. Minimax 2 (B2).
3. Maximin = minimax = 2: stable; value 2.

#### Common mistakes

- Taking column minima instead of maxima for the column player.

### DF4 Dominance

*Refs: AQA DF4 · Edexcel D2 5.3 · OCR 7.08b. Level: AS & A (OCR).*

#### What you need to know

- For the row player, row i dominates row j if every entry in row i is ≥ the corresponding entry in row j; delete row j.
- For the column player (who wants small values), column i dominates column j if every entry in column i is ≤ the entry in column j; delete column j.

#### Worked example

In the game above, which rows and columns can be removed?

1. A3 (6, 2, 3) vs A2 (3, 1, 0): A3 dominates A2. Delete A2.
2. Columns (after deleting A2): B2 (2, 2) is ≤ B1 (4, 6) and ≤ B3 (5, 3). Delete B1 and B3.

### DF5 Optimal mixed strategies

*Refs: AQA DF5 · Edexcel D2 5.4 · OCR 7.08e. Level: AS & A (OCR).*

#### What you need to know

- With no stable solution, each player chooses strategies at random with set probabilities.
- 2 × n (or n × 2) game: let the row player use A1 with probability p. Write the expected gain against each column as a line in p, plot them for 0 ≤ p ≤ 1, and find the highest point of the lowest boundary. That gives p and the value.
- The opponent's strategy: use only the columns whose lines meet at the optimal point, and equate expected losses.

#### Worked example

| | B1 | B2 | B3 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A1 | 3 | −1 | 2 |
| A2 | −2 | 4 | 0 |

1. Expected gains: B1: 3p − 2(1 − p) = 5p − 2. B2: −p + 4(1 − p) = 4 − 5p. B3: 2p.
2. The lowest boundary is highest where 5p − 2 = 4 − 5p: p = 0.6, value 1. (At p = 0.6, the B3 line gives 1.2 > 1, so B3 is not involved.)
3. A plays A1 with probability 0.6. B mixes B1 and B2 with q: 3q − (1 − q) = −2q + 4(1 − q), so q = 0.5.

A 2 × 2 game with rows (2, −1) and (−3, 4).

1. 2p − 3(1 − p) = −p + 4(1 − p), so 10p = 7, p = 0.7. Value 0.5.

#### Common mistakes

- Choosing the highest point of the top boundary.

### DF6 Larger games as linear programmes

*Refs: AQA DF6 · Edexcel D2 5.5 · OCR 7.08f. Level: A-level only (OCR); option.*

#### What you need to know

- Add a constant k to every entry so all are positive (value becomes V + k).
- Row player uses probabilities p₁, p₂, p₃. Maximise v subject to v ≤ expected gain against each column, p₁ + p₂ + p₃ = 1 (or ≤ 1), pᵢ ≥ 0.
- Solve by simplex (with slack variables) and subtract k at the end.

#### Worked example

Formulate for the row player: rows (1, −1, 2), (−2, 3, 0), (0, 1, −1).

1. Add 3: rows (4, 2, 5), (1, 6, 3), (3, 4, 2).
2. Maximise P = v subject to v − 4p₁ − p₂ − 3p₃ ≤ 0, v − 2p₁ − 6p₂ − 4p₃ ≤ 0, v − 5p₁ − 3p₂ − 2p₃ ≤ 0, p₁ + p₂ + p₃ ≤ 1, all ≥ 0.
3. The value of the original game is v − 3.

### Nash equilibrium

*Refs: OCR 7.08d. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- In a game that is not zero-sum, each cell lists both players' pay-offs. A Nash equilibrium is a pair of strategies where neither player can do better by changing their own strategy alone.

#### Worked example

| | Y1 | Y2 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| X1 | (3, 3) | (0, 5) |
| X2 | (5, 0) | (1, 1) |

1. Against Y1, X prefers X2 (5 > 3); against Y2, X2 (1 > 0). Similarly Y prefers Y2 either way.
2. (X2, Y2) with pay-offs (1, 1) is the Nash equilibrium, even though (X1, Y1) is better for both.

### Check questions

1. Show that the game with rows (5, 2, 4), (3, 1, 6), (6, 3, 7) has a stable solution and find its value. [4 marks]
2. Reduce rows (2, 4, 1), (3, 5, 2), (1, 0, 4) by dominance, then find the row player's optimal mixed strategy. [6 marks]
3. Explain what "zero-sum" means. [1 mark]
4. Formulate as an LP the column player's problem for the game with rows (2, −1) and (−3, 4). [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Row minima 2, 1, 3: maximin 3 (row 3) (1). Column maxima 6, 3, 7: minimax 3 (col 2) (1). Equal, so stable (1); value 3 (1).
2. Row 2 dominates row 1 (1). Rows (3, 5, 2), (1, 0, 4). No column dominates another, so use the graphical method with p = P(row (3, 5, 2)) (1). Lines: C1 2p + 1, C2 5p, C3 4 − 2p (where p = P(row 2)) (2). Optimal where 2p + 1 = 4 − 2p, p = 0.75, value 2.5 (check C2: 3.75 ≥ 2.5) (2).
3. The two players' pay-offs add to zero: one player's gain is the other's loss (1).
4. Add 4: rows become (6, 3) and (1, 8) (1). With column probabilities q₁, q₂: minimise v subject to the row player's expected gain from each row being at most v: 6q₁ + 3q₂ ≤ v and q₁ + 8q₂ ≤ v, q₁ + q₂ = 1, q₁, q₂ ≥ 0 (2). Value v − 4 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/26-sequences-and-recurrence-relations.md -->

## Sequences and recurrence relations

This file covers general sequences and their behaviour (convergent, divergent, periodic, oscillating), limits, the Fibonacci numbers, solving first and second order linear recurrence relations (homogeneous and non-homogeneous), proving closed forms by induction, and modelling with recurrence relations.

### Overview

Option content. Sequences and series from A-level Maths are in [../maths/08-sequences-and-series.md](../maths/08-sequences-and-series.md); induction in [01-proof-by-induction.md](01-proof-by-induction.md). Solving recurrences mirrors solving differential equations ([17-differential-equations-second-order-and-oscillations.md](17-differential-equations-second-order-and-oscillations.md)): auxiliary equation, complementary function, particular solution.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel FP2 6.1 to 6.3 (first and second order, proof by induction); Edexcel D2 6.1, 6.2 (the same skills in decision-maths modelling contexts, first and second order).
- OCR 8.01a to 8.01i includes the behaviour of sequences, limits and the Fibonacci numbers.
- MEI Xs1 to Xs9 includes modelling, verifying a given solution, first and second order linear equations (homogeneous and not), and commenting on long-term behaviour.

### Sequences and their behaviour

*Refs: OCR 8.01a, 8.01b, 8.01c, 8.01d, 8.01e · MEI Xs1, Xs2, Xs3, Xs4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A sequence may be given by a formula uₙ = f(n) or by a recurrence such as u_{n+1} = f(uₙ).
- Language: increasing, decreasing, periodic (repeats with period k), oscillating, convergent (tends to a limit), divergent.
- If u_{n+1} = f(uₙ) converges to L (and f is continuous), then L = f(L).
- Fibonacci: F₁ = F₂ = 1, F_{n+2} = F_{n+1} + Fₙ. The ratio F_{n+1}/Fₙ tends to the golden ratio (1 + √5)/2.

#### Worked example

uₙ₊₁ = ½uₙ + 3, u₁ = 2. Describe the behaviour and find the limit.

1. Terms 2, 4, 5, 5.5, 5.75: increasing, approaching a limit.
2. L = ½L + 3, so L = 6.

uₙ₊₁ = 1/(1 − uₙ), u₁ = 2. Show the sequence is periodic.

1. u₂ = −1, u₃ = ½, u₄ = 2. Period 3.

#### Common mistakes

- Assuming a limit exists just because L = f(L) has a solution.

### First order linear recurrence relations

*Refs: Edexcel FP2 6.1, 6.2, D2 6.1, 6.2 · OCR 8.01f, 8.01h · MEI Xs5, Xs6. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- u_{n+1} = a uₙ has solution uₙ = Caⁿ.
- u_{n+1} = a uₙ + f(n): general solution = complementary function Caⁿ + a particular solution. Try a constant for constant f, a linear polynomial for linear f, and kbⁿ for bⁿ (use knbⁿ if b = a).
- Use the initial condition last.

#### Worked example

Solve u_{n+1} = 3uₙ + 2, u₁ = 1.

1. CF: C × 3ⁿ. Particular: constant k = 3k + 2, k = −1.
2. uₙ = C × 3ⁿ − 1. u₁ = 3C − 1 = 1, C = ⅔.
3. uₙ = 2 × 3^{n−1} − 1.

Solve u_{n+1} = 2uₙ + n, u₀ = 1.

1. CF: C × 2ⁿ. Try pn + q: p(n + 1) + q = 2pn + 2q + n, so p = −1 and p + q = 2q, q = −1.
2. uₙ = C × 2ⁿ − n − 1. u₀ = C − 1 = 1, C = 2.
3. uₙ = 2^{n+1} − n − 1. Check u₁ = 2 × 1 + 0 = 2 and 4 − 2 = 2 ✓.

A loan of £10 000 has 1% interest added each month, then £200 is repaid. Find when it is paid off.

1. D_{n+1} = 1.01Dₙ − 200, D₀ = 10 000.
2. Particular: k = 1.01k − 200, k = 20 000. Dₙ = C(1.01)ⁿ + 20 000, C = −10 000.
3. Dₙ = 0 when 1.01ⁿ = 2, n = ln 2/ln 1.01 = 69.7. Paid off in the 70th month.

#### Common mistakes

- Applying the initial condition to the CF before adding the particular solution.

### Second order linear recurrence relations

*Refs: Edexcel FP2 6.1, 6.2, D2 6.2 · OCR 8.01g, 8.01i · MEI Xs7, Xs8, Xs9. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- u_{n+2} + a u_{n+1} + b uₙ = 0 has auxiliary equation m² + am + b = 0.

| Roots | Solution |
| --- | --- |
| distinct α, β | uₙ = Aαⁿ + Bβⁿ |
| repeated α | uₙ = (A + Bn)αⁿ |

- For a non-homogeneous right side f(n), add a particular solution, as for first order.
- Complex roots give oscillating (possibly periodic) behaviour.

#### Worked example

Solve u_{n+2} = 5u_{n+1} − 6uₙ, u₀ = 1, u₁ = 4.

1. m² − 5m + 6 = 0, m = 2, 3. uₙ = A2ⁿ + B3ⁿ.
2. A + B = 1, 2A + 3B = 4. B = 2, A = −1.
3. uₙ = 2 × 3ⁿ − 2ⁿ.

Solve u_{n+2} = 4u_{n+1} − 4uₙ, u₀ = 1, u₁ = 6.

1. (m − 2)² = 0. uₙ = (A + Bn)2ⁿ. A = 1; 2(1 + B) = 6, B = 2.
2. uₙ = (1 + 2n)2ⁿ.

Find a closed form for the Fibonacci numbers.

1. m² = m + 1, m = (1 ± √5)/2 = φ, ψ.
2. Fₙ = Aφⁿ + Bψⁿ with F₀ = 0, F₁ = 1: A = −B = 1/√5.
3. Fₙ = (φⁿ − ψⁿ)/√5. Since |ψ| < 1, F_{n+1}/Fₙ → φ.

#### Common mistakes

- Using n rather than n + 1 as the exponent when the sequence starts at u₁ (fit the constants to the given starting index).

### Proving closed forms by induction

*Refs: Edexcel FP2 6.3 · OCR 8.01b · MEI Xs4. Level: A-level only.*

#### Worked example

uₙ₊₁ = 3uₙ + 2, u₁ = 1. Prove that uₙ = 2 × 3^{n−1} − 1.

1. n = 1: 2 − 1 = 1 ✓.
2. Assume uₖ = 2 × 3^{k−1} − 1. Then u_{k+1} = 6 × 3^{k−1} − 3 + 2 = 2 × 3ᵏ − 1 ✓.
3. True for all n ≥ 1.

### Check questions

1. Solve u_{n+1} = 4uₙ − 3, u₁ = 3. [4 marks]
2. Solve u_{n+1} = 2uₙ + 3ⁿ, u₀ = 2. [5 marks]
3. Solve u_{n+2} = u_{n+1} + 6uₙ, u₁ = 1, u₂ = 13. [5 marks]
4. Solve u_{n+2} − 6u_{n+1} + 9uₙ = 0, u₀ = 2, u₁ = 9, and prove your answer by induction. [8 marks]
5. A population of fish is modelled by P_{n+1} = 1.2Pₙ − 500, where Pₙ is the population after n years, P₀ = 2000. Solve the recurrence, and comment on the long-term prediction. [5 marks]
6. Show that the sequence u_{n+1} = (uₙ + 3/uₙ)/2, u₁ = 2, appears to converge, and find the limit. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. CF C4ⁿ, particular 1 (2). uₙ = C4ⁿ + 1, u₁ = 4C + 1 = 3, C = ½ (1). uₙ = 2 × 4^{n−1} + 1 (1).
2. CF C2ⁿ (1). Try k3ⁿ: 3k = 2k + 1, k = 1 (2). uₙ = C2ⁿ + 3ⁿ, C = 1 (1). uₙ = 2ⁿ + 3ⁿ (1).
3. m² − m − 6 = 0, m = 3, −2 (2). 3A − 2B = 1, 9A + 4B = 13 (1). A = 1, B = 1 (1). uₙ = 3ⁿ + (−2)ⁿ (1).
4. (m − 3)² = 0, uₙ = (A + Bn)3ⁿ (2). A = 2, 3(2 + B) = 9, B = 1 (1). uₙ = (n + 2)3ⁿ (1). Induction (strong, two base cases n = 0, 1 ✓) (1). Assume for k and k + 1: u_{k+2} = 6(k + 3)3^{k+1} − 9(k + 2)3ᵏ = 3ᵏ[18k + 54 − 9k − 18] = 3ᵏ(9k + 36) = (k + 4)3^{k+2} (2). Conclusion (1).
5. Particular k = 1.2k − 500, k = 2500 (1). Pₙ = C(1.2)ⁿ + 2500, C = −500 (1). Pₙ = 2500 − 500(1.2)ⁿ (1). Becomes negative after about 9 years (1.2ⁿ = 5, n ≈ 8.8), so the fish die out; the model fails once P < 0 (2).
6. Terms 2, 1.75, 1.7321... (1). L = (L + 3/L)/2 gives L² = 3 (1), L = √3 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/25-number-theory.md -->

## Number theory

This file covers number bases and divisibility tests, the division algorithm, Euclid's algorithm and Bezout's identity, primes and Euclid's lemma, modular arithmetic, linear and simultaneous congruences, Fermat's little theorem, orders and primitive roots, quadratic residues, and (MEI) Euler's totient, Wilson's theorem, Pythagorean triples and Pell's equation.

### Overview

Option content. Proof by induction ([01-proof-by-induction.md](01-proof-by-induction.md)) and groups ([24-groups.md](24-groups.md)) link closely: the non-zero residues mod p form a group under multiplication.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel FP2 5.1 to 5.6 (Euclid's algorithm, Bezout, modular arithmetic, Fermat's little theorem, divisibility tests, congruences). FP2 5.7 (counting) is in [59-combinatorics.md](59-combinatorics.md).
- OCR 8.02a to 8.02o goes furthest on theory: number bases, quadratic residues, simultaneous congruences, orders and primitive roots.
- MEI TT1 to TT10 (Further Pure with Technology) is computational: writing short programs, Euler's totient, Wilson's theorem, Pythagorean triples, Pell's equation and other Diophantine equations. Y436 is being withdrawn (last exam 2028).

### Number bases and divisibility tests

*Refs: Edexcel FP2 5.5 · OCR 8.02a, 8.02b, 8.02c. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A number in base b: (dₖ...d₁d₀)_b = dₖbᵏ + ... + d₁b + d₀.
- Tests: 2 (last digit even), 3 and 9 (digit sum), 4 (last two digits), 5, 6 (2 and 3), 11 (alternating digit sum), and be able to prove them with congruences.

#### Explained

10 ≡ 1 (mod 9), so every power of 10 is ≡ 1 and a number is congruent to its digit sum mod 9 (and mod 3). 10 ≡ −1 (mod 11), so powers alternate in sign, giving the alternating sum test.

#### Worked example

Write 213 in base 5 and (1011)₂ in base 10.

1. 213 = 1 × 125 + 3 × 25 + 2 × 5 + 3, so (1323)₅.
2. (1011)₂ = 8 + 2 + 1 = 11.

Is 918 082 divisible by 11?

1. Alternating sum from the right: 2 − 8 + 0 − 8 + 1 − 9 = −22, a multiple of 11. Yes.

#### Common mistakes

- Starting the alternating sum from the wrong end with a sign error (either end works if you are consistent; the result is ± the same).

### Euclid's algorithm and Bezout's identity

*Refs: Edexcel FP2 5.1, 5.2 · OCR 8.02d, 8.02i, 8.02j, 8.02k · MEI TT3. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Division algorithm: a = qb + r with 0 ≤ r < b.
- Euclid's algorithm: gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, r); repeat until the remainder is 0.
- Bezout's identity: there are integers x, y with ax + by = gcd(a, b). Find them by working back through the algorithm.
- Euclid's lemma: if p is prime and p | ab, then p | a or p | b. Unique prime factorisation follows.

#### Worked example

Find gcd(1071, 462) and integers x, y with 1071x + 462y = gcd.

1. 1071 = 2 × 462 + 147. 462 = 3 × 147 + 21. 147 = 7 × 21 + 0. gcd = 21.
2. 21 = 462 − 3 × 147 = 462 − 3(1071 − 2 × 462) = 7 × 462 − 3 × 1071.
3. x = −3, y = 7.

#### Common mistakes
