- Losing track of which numbers to substitute back; keep each step as "remainder = a − qb".

### Modular arithmetic and congruences

*Refs: Edexcel FP2 5.3, 5.6 · OCR 8.02e, 8.02f, 8.02h · MEI TT4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- a ≡ b (mod n) means n | (a − b). Congruences can be added, subtracted and multiplied.
- ax ≡ b (mod n) has a solution if and only if gcd(a, n) | b. If gcd(a, n) = 1 there is a unique solution mod n: multiply by the inverse of a (found by Bezout).
- (OCR) Simultaneous congruences with coprime moduli have a unique solution modulo the product (Chinese remainder theorem).

#### Worked example

Solve 7x ≡ 5 (mod 26).

1. Inverse of 7 mod 26: 7 × 15 = 105 = 4 × 26 + 1, so 7⁻¹ ≡ 15.
2. x ≡ 15 × 5 = 75 ≡ 23 (mod 26).

Solve 6x ≡ 4 (mod 10).

1. gcd(6, 10) = 2 divides 4. Divide through: 3x ≡ 2 (mod 5).
2. 3⁻¹ ≡ 2 (mod 5), so x ≡ 4 (mod 5): x ≡ 4 or 9 (mod 10).

(OCR) Find x with x ≡ 2 (mod 3), x ≡ 3 (mod 5), x ≡ 2 (mod 7).

1. x ≡ 2 (mod 21) from the first and third (both ≡ 2, and 3, 7 coprime).
2. x = 2 + 21k ≡ 3 (mod 5): 2 + k ≡ 3, k ≡ 1. x = 23.
3. x ≡ 23 (mod 105).

#### Common mistakes

- "Dividing" a congruence by a number not coprime to the modulus.

### Fermat's little theorem, orders and primitive roots

*Refs: Edexcel FP2 5.4 · OCR 8.02l, 8.02m, 8.02n, 8.02o, 8.02g · MEI TT5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- If p is prime and p ∤ a, then a^{p−1} ≡ 1 (mod p). Also aᵖ ≡ a (mod p) for all a.
- (OCR) The order of a mod p is the least n with aⁿ ≡ 1; it divides p − 1. A primitive root has order p − 1.
- (OCR) a is a quadratic residue mod p if x² ≡ a has a solution.

#### Explained

Fermat's theorem lets you cut huge powers down: reduce the exponent mod (p − 1). One proof uses the binomial theorem to show (a + 1)ᵖ ≡ aᵖ + 1 (mod p) (because p divides each C(p, r) for 0 < r < p) and induction on a.

#### Worked example

Find 3¹⁰⁰ mod 7 and 7²²² mod 11.

1. 3⁶ ≡ 1 (mod 7). 100 = 6 × 16 + 4, so 3¹⁰⁰ ≡ 3⁴ = 81 ≡ 4.
2. 7¹⁰ ≡ 1 (mod 11). 222 = 220 + 2, so 7²²² ≡ 49 ≡ 5.

(OCR) Show that 2 is a primitive root mod 11, and list the quadratic residues mod 11.

1. Powers of 2 mod 11: 2, 4, 8, 5, 10, 9, 7, 3, 6, 1. Order 10 = p − 1.
2. Squares of 1 to 10 mod 11: 1, 4, 9, 5, 3, 3, 5, 9, 4, 1. Residues {1, 3, 4, 5, 9}.

#### Common mistakes

- Applying Fermat when p divides a.

### Further results with technology

*Refs: MEI TT1, TT2, TT6, TT7, TT8, TT9, TT10. Level: A-level only (option content).*

#### What you need to know

- Write, adapt and interpret short programs (for example trial division, or searching for solutions), and know their limits (run time, number size).
- Euler's totient φ(n) counts integers 1 ≤ k ≤ n coprime to n; a^{φ(n)} ≡ 1 (mod n) when gcd(a, n) = 1.
- Wilson's theorem: p is prime if and only if (p − 1)! ≡ −1 (mod p).
- Pythagorean triples: (m² − n², 2mn, m² + n²).
- Pell's equation x² − dy² = 1: from a smallest solution (x₁, y₁), further solutions come from (x₁ + y₁√d)ᵏ.

#### Worked example

Find φ(36) and use it to find 5¹⁰⁰ mod 36.

1. 36 = 2²3²; φ = 36 × ½ × ⅔ = 12.
2. 100 = 8 × 12 + 4. 5⁴ = 625 = 17 × 36 + 13. Answer 13.

Find two solutions of x² − 2y² = 1.

1. (3, 2) works: 9 − 8 = 1.
2. (3 + 2√2)² = 17 + 12√2, giving (17, 12): 289 − 288 = 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Using a^{n−1} ≡ 1 (mod n) for composite n.

### Check questions

1. Use Euclid's algorithm to find gcd(252, 198) and integers x and y with 252x + 198y = gcd. [5 marks]
2. Prove that a number is divisible by 9 if and only if its digit sum is divisible by 9. [3 marks]
3. Solve 11x ≡ 4 (mod 17). [3 marks]
4. Find the remainder when 2⁵⁰ is divided by 13. [3 marks]
5. (OCR) Solve x ≡ 1 (mod 4), x ≡ 2 (mod 5), x ≡ 3 (mod 9). [4 marks]
6. Show that 4x ≡ 6 (mod 10) has solutions and find them all modulo 10. [3 marks]
7. (MEI) Use Wilson's theorem to show that 10! ≡ −1 (mod 11). [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 252 = 1 × 198 + 54; 198 = 3 × 54 + 36; 54 = 1 × 36 + 18; 36 = 2 × 18 (2). gcd 18 (1). 18 = 54 − 36 = 54 − (198 − 3 × 54) = 4 × 54 − 198 = 4 × 252 − 5 × 198 (1). x = 4, y = −5 (1).
2. 10 ≡ 1 (mod 9), so 10ᵏ ≡ 1 (1). N = Σdₖ10ᵏ ≡ Σdₖ (mod 9) (1). So 9 | N ⇔ 9 | digit sum (1).
3. 11⁻¹ mod 17: 11 × 14 = 154 = 9 × 17 + 1 (1). x ≡ 14 × 4 = 56 ≡ 5 (mod 17) (2).
4. 2¹² ≡ 1 (mod 13) (1). 2⁵⁰ = (2¹²)⁴ × 2² (1) ≡ 4 (1).
5. From mod 4 and 5: x ≡ 17 (mod 20) (2). 17 + 20k ≡ 3 (mod 9): 8 + 2k ≡ 3, 2k ≡ 4, k ≡ 2 (1). x ≡ 57 (mod 180) (1).
6. gcd(4, 10) = 2 | 6 (1). 2x ≡ 3 (mod 5), x ≡ 4 (mod 5) (1). x ≡ 4 or 9 (mod 10) (1).
7. 11 is prime (1), so (11 − 1)! ≡ −1 (mod 11) (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/24-groups.md -->

## Groups

This file covers binary operations and their properties, Cayley tables, the group axioms, standard examples of groups, the order of a group and of an element, subgroups, cyclic groups and generators, Lagrange's theorem, and isomorphism.

### Overview

Option content on every board. It uses modular arithmetic ([25-number-theory.md](25-number-theory.md)), matrices ([05-matrices-operations-and-transformations.md](05-matrices-operations-and-transformations.md)) and roots of unity ([04-complex-numbers-de-moivre-exponential-form-and-nth-roots.md](04-complex-numbers-de-moivre-exponential-form-and-nth-roots.md)).

#### Board differences

- AQA DG1 to DG12 (in the Discrete option); Edexcel FP2 1.1 to 1.5; OCR 8.03a to 8.03m; MEI XS1, XS2, Xa1 to Xa8.
- AQA DG2, DG3 and DG5 ask you to prove commutativity, associativity and the existence of an identity for given operations.
- OCR 8.03i expects familiarity with all groups up to order 8 and 8.03m with groups given by generators and relations (such as ⟨a, b : a⁴ = b² = e, ba = a³b⟩).
- Edexcel FP2 1.5 limits isomorphism to groups of order at most 8.

### DG1 Binary operations and their properties

*Refs: AQA DG1, DG2, DG3, DG4, DG5, DG6 · OCR 8.03a, 8.03b · MEI XS1, XS2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A binary operation ∗ on a set S combines two elements a, b to give a ∗ b. S is closed under ∗ if a ∗ b ∈ S for all a, b ∈ S.
- Commutative: a ∗ b = b ∗ a for all a, b. Associative: (a ∗ b) ∗ c = a ∗ (b ∗ c) for all a, b, c.
- Identity e: e ∗ a = a ∗ e = a for all a. Inverse of a: a⁻¹ with a ∗ a⁻¹ = a⁻¹ ∗ a = e.
- A Cayley table shows every product; row element first.
- Examples: addition and multiplication modulo n; matrix multiplication; composition of functions or transformations.

#### Explained

To prove a property holds you need a general argument; to show it fails you need one counter-example. Commutativity shows in a Cayley table as symmetry about the leading diagonal. Associativity cannot be seen easily from a table; it is usually inherited (from ordinary multiplication, matrix multiplication or composition of functions).

#### Worked example

On ℝ, define a ∗ b = a + b + ab. Is ∗ commutative and associative? Find the identity and the inverse of a.

1. a ∗ b = a + b + ab = b ∗ a: commutative.
2. (a ∗ b) ∗ c = a + b + ab + c + (a + b + ab)c = a + b + c + ab + bc + ca + abc. This is symmetric in a, b, c, so it equals a ∗ (b ∗ c): associative.
3. a ∗ e = a + e + ae = a needs e(1 + a) = 0 for all a, so e = 0.
4. a ∗ x = 0 gives x = −a/(1 + a), which exists unless a = −1.

#### Common mistakes

- Checking one example and claiming a property holds in general.

### DG8 The group axioms and examples

*Refs: AQA DG7, DG8, DG9 · Edexcel FP2 1.1, 1.2 · OCR 8.03c, 8.03d · MEI Xa1, Xa2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

A group (G, ∗) is a set with a binary operation satisfying:

1. **Closure**: a ∗ b ∈ G.
2. **Associativity**.
3. **Identity**: there is e ∈ G with e ∗ a = a ∗ e = a.
4. **Inverses**: every a has a⁻¹ ∈ G.

- Abelian group: also commutative.
- In a finite group's Cayley table each element appears exactly once in every row and column (Latin square property).
- Standard groups: (ℤ, +); (ℤₙ, + mod n); {1, 2, ..., p − 1} under × mod p for p prime; nth roots of unity under ×; symmetries of a regular n-gon (order 2n); non-singular 2 × 2 matrices under multiplication.

#### Explained

The identity is unique and each element has exactly one inverse. Cancellation follows: if a ∗ b = a ∗ c then b = c. That is why rows of a Cayley table cannot repeat entries.

{0, 1, ..., n − 1} under × mod n is not a group (0 has no inverse). Even without 0, × mod n fails when n is not prime: 2 × 2 = 0 mod 4.

#### Worked example

Show that G = {1, 5, 7, 11} under multiplication modulo 12 is a group.

1. Cayley table products: 5 × 5 = 25 ≡ 1, 7 × 7 = 49 ≡ 1, 11 × 11 = 121 ≡ 1, 5 × 7 = 35 ≡ 11, 5 × 11 = 55 ≡ 7, 7 × 11 = 77 ≡ 5. Closed.
2. Associative: inherited from multiplication of integers.
3. Identity 1. Each element is its own inverse.
4. So G is a group (abelian, every non-identity element of order 2).

Describe the group of symmetries of an equilateral triangle.

1. Three rotations (0°, 120°, 240°) and three reflections: order 6.
2. Not abelian: a rotation followed by a reflection differs from the reflection followed by the rotation.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to justify associativity.
- Claiming a set is a group without checking closure.

### DG7 Order, subgroups and Lagrange's theorem

*Refs: AQA DG7, DG10 · Edexcel FP2 1.3, 1.4 · OCR 8.03e, 8.03f, 8.03k · MEI Xa3, Xa4, Xa5, Xa6. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The order of a group is the number of elements, |G|.
- The order (period) of an element a is the least positive n with aⁿ = e.
- A subgroup H of G is a subset that is a group under the same operation. Every group has the trivial subgroup {e} and G itself; others are proper non-trivial subgroups.
- **Lagrange's theorem**: for a finite group, the order of a subgroup divides the order of the group.
- Corollary: the order of every element divides |G|. A group of prime order is cyclic and has no proper non-trivial subgroups.

#### Explained

To test a subset H of a finite group, check closure (associativity is inherited, and in a finite group closure gives identity and inverses). Lagrange's theorem tells you which subgroup sizes to look for, but not that a subgroup of each size exists. For example, the group of rotations of a regular tetrahedron has order 12 but no subgroup of order 6.

#### Worked example

Find the orders of the elements of (ℤ₈, + mod 8) and list its subgroups.

1. 0 has order 1; 4 has order 2; 2 and 6 have order 4; 1, 3, 5, 7 have order 8.
2. Subgroups: {0}, {0, 4}, {0, 2, 4, 6}, ℤ₈ (orders 1, 2, 4, 8, all dividing 8).

A group has order 15. What are the possible orders of its elements?

1. Divisors of 15: 1, 3, 5, 15.

#### Common mistakes

- Concluding that a subgroup of every order dividing |G| must exist.

### DG11 Cyclic groups and generators

*Refs: AQA DG9, DG11 · Edexcel FP2 1.3 · OCR 8.03g, 8.03h, 8.03i, 8.03j, 8.03m · MEI Xa3, Xa4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- G is cyclic if some element g generates it: every element is a power of g. g is a generator.
- A cyclic group of order n has an element of order n. Cyclic groups are abelian.
- (OCR) Groups up to order 8: orders 1, 2, 3, 5, 7 are cyclic only; order 4 is cyclic C₄ or the Klein group V₄; order 6 is C₆ or the triangle symmetry group D₃; order 8 includes C₈, C₄ × C₂, C₂ × C₂ × C₂, D₄ and the quaternion group Q₈.
- (OCR 8.03m) Groups can be defined by generators and relations.

#### Worked example

Show that {1, 2, 3, 4} under × mod 5 is cyclic, and find all generators.

1. Powers of 2: 2, 4, 8 ≡ 3, 6 ≡ 1. All four elements, so 2 is a generator.
2. Powers of 3: 3, 4, 2, 1. Generator. 4 has order 2 (16 ≡ 1), not a generator.
3. Generators 2 and 3.

The group ⟨a, b : a³ = b² = e, ba = a²b⟩ has order 6. Show it is not abelian.

1. ba = a²b ≠ ab (else a² = a, a = e). So ab ≠ ba.

#### Common mistakes

- Thinking every abelian group is cyclic (V₄ is abelian but not cyclic).

### DG12 Isomorphism

*Refs: AQA DG12 · Edexcel FP2 1.5 · OCR 8.03l · MEI Xa7, Xa8. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- G and H are isomorphic if there is a one-to-one correspondence φ: G → H with φ(a ∗ b) = φ(a) ∘ φ(b). The Cayley tables then match after relabelling.
- Isomorphic groups have the same order and the same number of elements of each order. To show two groups are not isomorphic, find a structural difference (for example one is cyclic and the other is not).

#### Worked example

Show that ({1, 2, 3, 4}, × mod 5) is isomorphic to (ℤ₄, + mod 4).

1. Both are cyclic of order 4. Map 2ᵏ ↦ k: 1 ↦ 0, 2 ↦ 1, 4 ↦ 2, 3 ↦ 3.
2. 2ʲ × 2ᵏ = 2^{j+k} corresponds to j + k (mod 4), so the operation is preserved.

Are {1, 5, 7, 11} under × mod 12 and (ℤ₄, +) isomorphic?

1. The first has no element of order 4; ℤ₄ has 1 of order 4. Not isomorphic.

#### Common mistakes

- Matching elements without checking that the operation is preserved.

### Check questions

1. a ∗ b = |a − b| on the integers. Show that ∗ is commutative but not associative. [3 marks]
2. Show that the set of matrices [[1, n], [0, 1]], n ∈ ℤ, forms a group under matrix multiplication. [5 marks]
3. Construct the Cayley table for {1, 3, 7, 9} under × mod 10, show it is a group, and find the order of each element. [6 marks]
4. G is a group of order 12. List the possible orders of its subgroups, and explain why G cannot have an element of order 5. [3 marks]
5. Show that the fourth roots of unity {1, i, −1, −i} under multiplication form a cyclic group, and state an isomorphism with (ℤ₄, +). [4 marks]
6. Explain why the group of symmetries of a square (order 8) is not isomorphic to (ℤ₈, +). [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. |a − b| = |b − a| (1). Counter-example: (1 ∗ 2) ∗ 3 = 1 ∗ 3 = 2 but 1 ∗ (2 ∗ 3) = 1 ∗ 1 = 0 (2).
2. Product [[1, m + n], [0, 1]], closed (1). Matrix multiplication is associative (1). Identity n = 0 (1). Inverse n ↦ −n (1). All conditions met (1).
3. Table correct (2). Closed, identity 1, inverses 3 ↔ 7, 9 ↔ 9, associativity from multiplication (2). Orders: 1 (1), 9 (2), 3 and 7 (4) (2).
4. 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12 (2). 5 does not divide 12, so by Lagrange no element has order 5 (1).
5. i generates: i, −1, −i, 1 (2). iᵏ ↦ k (mod 4) (2).
6. ℤ₈ has an element of order 8; no symmetry of a square has order 8 (rotations have order at most 4, reflections order 2) (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/28-multivariable-calculus.md -->

## Multivariable calculus

This file covers functions of two variables as surfaces, sections and contours, first and second partial derivatives, the gradient vector, tangent planes and normals, and finding and classifying stationary points.

### Overview

Option content on OCR (Additional Pure) and MEI (Extra Pure). It uses vectors and planes ([10-vectors-lines-planes-and-scalar-product.md](10-vectors-lines-planes-and-scalar-product.md)) and the usual one-variable rules of differentiation.

#### Board differences

- OCR 8.05a to 8.05g: surfaces z = f(x, y) and g(x, y, z) = c, sections and contours, partial derivatives, stationary points, tangent planes, and the nature of stationary points (using the Hessian determinant).
- MEI Xc1 to Xc7: surfaces, contours and sections, first and second partial derivatives, grad g and normals, tangent planes and stationary points.
- OCR 8.05b (Stage 2) extends to functions of more than two variables including exponentials, logs and trig.

### Surfaces, sections and contours

*Refs: OCR 8.05a, 8.05b, 8.05c · MEI Xc1, Xc2, Xc5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- z = f(x, y) defines a surface in 3D. So does g(x, y, z) = c.
- A section is the curve where the surface meets a plane such as x = k or y = k. A contour is the curve f(x, y) = k (the section by the plane z = k, seen from above).

#### Worked example

Describe the sections and contours of z = x² + 2y².

1. Section y = 0: z = x², a parabola. Section x = 0: z = 2y², a narrower parabola.
2. Contours z = k > 0: x² + 2y² = k, ellipses. The surface is a bowl (an elliptic paraboloid).

Describe the surface z = x² − y².

1. Sections y = 0: z = x² (up); x = 0: z = −y² (down). A saddle shape.
2. Contours x² − y² = k: hyperbolas, and the lines y = ±x for k = 0.

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing sections (vertical slices) with contours (horizontal slices).

### Partial derivatives

*Refs: OCR 8.05d · MEI Xc3, Xc4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- ∂f/∂x: differentiate with respect to x, treating y as constant. ∂f/∂y similarly.
- Second derivatives f_xx, f_yy, f_xy = f_yx (equal for the functions met here).
- ∂f/∂x at a point is the gradient of the section y = constant through that point.

#### Worked example

f = xe^{xy}. Find f_x, f_y and f_xy.

1. f_x = e^{xy} + xye^{xy} (product rule).
2. f_y = x²e^{xy}.
3. f_xy = ∂/∂y(f_x) = xe^{xy} + xe^{xy} + x²ye^{xy} = x(2 + xy)e^{xy}. (f_yx = 2xe^{xy} + x²ye^{xy}, the same.)

#### Common mistakes

- Treating y as a function of x.

### Tangent planes, normals and grad

*Refs: OCR 8.05f · MEI Xc5, Xc6, Xc7. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Tangent plane to z = f(x, y) at (a, b, f(a, b)):

```latex
z - f(a,b) = f_x(a,b)(x-a) + f_y(a,b)(y-b)
```

- For a surface g(x, y, z) = c, grad g = (g_x, g_y, g_z) is normal to the surface. The tangent plane at P has normal (grad g)_P.
- The surface z = f(x, y) is g = f(x, y) − z = 0, with normal (f_x, f_y, −1).

#### Worked example

Find the tangent plane to z = x² + 2y² at (1, 1, 3).

1. f_x = 2x = 2, f_y = 4y = 4.
2. z − 3 = 2(x − 1) + 4(y − 1), so z = 2x + 4y − 3.

Find the tangent plane and normal line to x² + y² + z² = 9 at (1, 2, 2).

1. grad g = (2x, 2y, 2z) = (2, 4, 4), or (1, 2, 2).
2. Plane: x + 2y + 2z = 1 + 4 + 4 = 9.
3. Normal line: r = (1, 2, 2) + t(1, 2, 2), through the centre, as expected for a sphere.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting the −1 in the normal to z = f(x, y).

### Stationary points

*Refs: OCR 8.05e, 8.05g · MEI Xc7. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Stationary points satisfy f_x = 0 and f_y = 0 (the tangent plane is horizontal).
- (OCR 8.05g) Classify with D = f_xx f_yy − (f_xy)²:
  - D > 0 and f_xx > 0: minimum; D > 0 and f_xx < 0: maximum;
  - D < 0: saddle point; D = 0: test fails, look at sections.
- (MEI) Classify by considering sections or contours near the point.

#### Worked example

Find and classify the stationary points of f = x³ + y³ − 3xy.

1. f_x = 3x² − 3y = 0, f_y = 3y² − 3x = 0. So y = x², x = y², giving x⁴ = x: (0, 0) and (1, 1).
2. f_xx = 6x, f_yy = 6y, f_xy = −3.
3. (0, 0): D = 0 − 9 < 0, saddle. (1, 1): D = 36 − 9 > 0, f_xx > 0, minimum, f = −1.

Find the stationary points of f = x²y + y² − 4y.

1. f_x = 2xy = 0, f_y = x² + 2y − 4 = 0.
2. x = 0: y = 2. y = 0: x = ±2. Points (0, 2, −4), (2, 0, 0), (−2, 0, 0).
3. f_xx = 2y, f_yy = 2, f_xy = 2x. At (0, 2): D = 8 > 0, f_xx > 0, minimum. At (±2, 0): D = −16, saddles.

#### Common mistakes

- Missing solutions by dividing by a variable that may be zero (factorise instead).

### Check questions

1. Describe the contours of z = 4 − x² − y² and sketch the surface. [3 marks]
2. f(x, y) = x² sin y + y³. Find f_x, f_y, f_xx, f_yy and f_xy. [5 marks]
3. Find the equation of the tangent plane to z = xy² at (2, 1, 2). [4 marks]
4. Find the equation of the tangent plane to x²/4 + y² + z²/9 = 3 at (2, 1, 3). [4 marks]
5. Find and classify the stationary points of f(x, y) = x² + xy + y² − 3x. [6 marks]
6. Find the stationary points of z = x³ − 12x + y² − 2y and classify them. [6 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Circles x² + y² = 4 − z, centre the z-axis, shrinking to a point at z = 4 (2). Dome (paraboloid opening downwards) (1).
2. f_x = 2x sin y (1). f_y = x² cos y + 3y² (1). f_xx = 2 sin y (1). f_yy = −x² sin y + 6y (1). f_xy = 2x cos y (1).
3. f_x = y² = 1, f_y = 2xy = 4 (2). z − 2 = (x − 2) + 4(y − 1) (1). z = x + 4y − 4 (1).
4. grad = (x/2, 2y, 2z/9) = (1, 2, ⅔) (2). Normal (3, 6, 2) (1). 3x + 6y + 2z = 6 + 6 + 6 = 18 (1).
5. 2x + y − 3 = 0, x + 2y = 0 (2). x = 2, y = −1 (1). f_xx = 2, f_yy = 2, f_xy = 1, D = 3 > 0 (2). Minimum at (2, −1), f = −3 (1).
6. 3x² − 12 = 0, 2y − 2 = 0 (1). (2, 1) and (−2, 1) (1). f_xx = 6x, f_yy = 2, f_xy = 0 (1). (2, 1): D = 24 > 0, minimum, z = −17 (1). (−2, 1): D = −24, saddle, z = 15 (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/20-arc-length-surface-area-and-reduction-formulae.md -->

## Arc length, surface area and reduction formulae

This file covers the length of a curve and the area of a surface of revolution, for curves in Cartesian and parametric form, and deriving and using reduction formulae for integrals.

### Overview

All A-level only. It uses integration by parts and substitution ([../maths/15-integration-techniques.md](../maths/15-integration-techniques.md)), parametric differentiation ([../maths/13-differentiation-further-techniques.md](../maths/13-differentiation-further-techniques.md)) and hyperbolic functions ([15-hyperbolic-functions.md](15-hyperbolic-functions.md)). Volumes of revolution are in [12-further-calculus-volumes-improper-integrals-and-mean-value.md](12-further-calculus-volumes-improper-integrals-and-mean-value.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA E7, E8 (compulsory); Edexcel FP2 2.1, 2.2; OCR 8.06a, 8.06b (Additional Pure); MEI TC8 (arc length only, Further Pure with Technology).
- Surfaces of revolution are about the x-axis on AQA and OCR; Edexcel FP2 includes rotation about the x-axis for Cartesian, parametric and (in some cases) polar curves. Polar arc length is Edexcel FP2 and MEI TC8 only.
- OCR 8.06a says "establish and use given reduction formulae".

### E7 Arc length

*Refs: AQA E7 · Edexcel FP2 2.2 · OCR 8.06b · MEI TC8. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
s = \int_a^b \sqrt{1 + \left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right)^2}\,dx
```

```latex
s = \int_{t_1}^{t_2} \sqrt{\left(\frac{dx}{dt}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{dy}{dt}\right)^2}\,dt
```

- (Edexcel FP2, MEI TC8) Polar: s = ∫√(r² + (dr/dθ)²) dθ.

#### Explained

A tiny piece of curve is nearly straight, with length √(δx² + δy²) by Pythagoras. Dividing inside by δx or δt and integrating gives the formulae.

The integrand is usually nasty unless the question is designed to make 1 + (dy/dx)² a perfect square, or something that integrates by a standard substitution. Look for that.

#### Worked example

Find the length of y = (2/3)x^{3/2} from x = 0 to x = 3.

1. dy/dx = x^{1/2}. 1 + (dy/dx)² = 1 + x.
2. s = ∫₀³ √(1 + x) dx = (2/3)[(1 + x)^{3/2}]₀³ = (2/3)(8 − 1) = 14/3.

Find the length of y = cosh x from x = 0 to x = ln 2.

1. 1 + sinh²x = cosh²x.
2. s = ∫₀^{ln 2} cosh x dx = sinh(ln 2) = (2 − ½)/2 = ¾.

Find the length of the curve x = t³, y = 3t² for 0 ≤ t ≤ 2.

1. (3t²)² + (6t)² = 9t²(t² + 4).
2. s = ∫₀² 3t√(t² + 4) dt = [(t² + 4)^{3/2}]₀² = 8^{3/2} − 8 = 16√2 − 8.

Find the total length of the astroid x = cos³t, y = sin³t.

1. (dx/dt)² + (dy/dt)² = 9cos⁴t sin²t + 9sin⁴t cos²t = 9sin²t cos²t.
2. For 0 ≤ t ≤ π/2, s = ∫ 3 sin t cos t dt = 3/2.
3. By symmetry, total length 4 × 3/2 = 6.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to square dy/dx inside the root.
- Taking √(sin²t cos²t) as sin t cos t on an interval where it is negative.

### E7 Area of a surface of revolution

*Refs: AQA E7 · Edexcel FP2 2.2 · OCR 8.06b. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

For rotation through 2π about the x-axis:

```latex
S = 2\pi\int_a^b y\sqrt{1 + \left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right)^2}\,dx = 2\pi\int_{t_1}^{t_2} y\sqrt{\left(\frac{dx}{dt}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{dy}{dt}\right)^2}\,dt
```

- About the y-axis, replace y by x in front: S = 2π∫x ds.

#### Explained

Each small piece of arc, length δs, sweeps out a thin band of radius y, with area about 2πy δs.

#### Worked example

Find the area of the surface formed when y = x³ for 0 ≤ x ≤ 1 is rotated about the x-axis.

1. S = 2π∫₀¹ x³√(1 + 9x⁴) dx. Let u = 1 + 9x⁴, du = 36x³ dx.
2. = 2π × (1/36)∫₁¹⁰ u^{1/2} du = (π/18) × (2/3)(10√10 − 1) = (π/27)(10√10 − 1).

The curve x = t², y = 2t, 0 ≤ t ≤ 1, is rotated about the x-axis. Find the surface area.

1. √(4t² + 4) = 2√(t² + 1).
2. S = 2π∫₀¹ 2t × 2√(t² + 1) dt = 8π[⅓(t² + 1)^{3/2}]₀¹ = (8π/3)(2√2 − 1).

Show that the surface area of a sphere of radius r is 4πr².

1. Rotate x = r cos θ, y = r sin θ, 0 ≤ θ ≤ π, about the x-axis. ds/dθ = r.
2. S = 2π∫₀^π r sin θ × r dθ = 2πr²[−cos θ]₀^π = 4πr².

#### Common mistakes

- Using y² (from the volume formula) instead of y.

### E8 Reduction formulae

*Refs: AQA E8 · Edexcel FP2 2.1 · OCR 8.06a. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A reduction formula links Iₙ (an integral involving a power n) to I_{n−1} or I_{n−2}.
- Derive it, usually by integration by parts; then apply it repeatedly from a value you can work out directly.

#### Explained

For ∫xⁿeˣ dx, parts with u = xⁿ lowers the power by 1. For ∫sinⁿx dx, write sinⁿx = sin^{n−1}x × sin x, use parts, then replace cos²x by 1 − sin²x so that Iₙ appears again on the right, and collect it on the left.

#### Worked example

Iₙ = ∫₀¹ xⁿeˣ dx. Show that Iₙ = e − nI_{n−1} and find I₄.

1. Parts: u = xⁿ, v′ = eˣ. Iₙ = [xⁿeˣ]₀¹ − n∫₀¹ x^{n−1}eˣ dx = e − nI_{n−1}.
2. I₀ = e − 1. I₁ = e − (e − 1) = 1. I₂ = e − 2. I₃ = e − 3(e − 2) = 6 − 2e. I₄ = e − 4(6 − 2e) = 9e − 24.

Iₙ = ∫₀^{π/2} sinⁿx dx. Show that Iₙ = ((n − 1)/n)I_{n−2} for n ≥ 2, and find I₆.

1. Iₙ = ∫ sin^{n−1}x sin x dx. Parts: u = sin^{n−1}x, v = −cos x.
2. Iₙ = [−sin^{n−1}x cos x]₀^{π/2} + (n − 1)∫ sin^{n−2}x cos²x dx = 0 + (n − 1)∫ sin^{n−2}x(1 − sin²x) dx.
3. Iₙ = (n − 1)(I_{n−2} − Iₙ), so nIₙ = (n − 1)I_{n−2}.
4. I₀ = π/2. I₆ = (5/6)(3/4)(1/2)(π/2) = 5π/32.

Jₙ = ∫₀^{π/2} xⁿ cos x dx. Show that Jₙ = (π/2)ⁿ − n(n − 1)J_{n−2} and find J₃.

1. Parts twice: Jₙ = [xⁿ sin x]₀^{π/2} − n∫x^{n−1} sin x dx = (π/2)ⁿ − n{[−x^{n−1} cos x]₀^{π/2} + (n − 1)J_{n−2}}.
2. The bracket is 0 for n ≥ 2. So Jₙ = (π/2)ⁿ − n(n − 1)J_{n−2}.
3. J₁ = [x sin x + cos x]₀^{π/2} = π/2 − 1. J₃ = π³/8 − 6(π/2 − 1) = π³/8 − 3π + 6.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting that the boundary term may not be zero.
- Stopping at a step whose integral you cannot do directly; work down to I₀ or I₁.

### Check questions

1. Find the length of y = ln(cos x) from x = 0 to x = π/4. [5 marks]
2. Find the length of the curve x = 3t², y = 2t³ for 0 ≤ t ≤ √3. [5 marks]
3. The arc of y = 2√x from x = 0 to x = 3 is rotated about the x-axis. Find the surface area. [5 marks]
4. Iₙ = ∫₀^∞ xⁿe^{−x} dx. Show that Iₙ = nI_{n−1} and deduce Iₙ = n!. [5 marks]
5. Iₙ = ∫₀^{π/4} tanⁿx dx. Show that Iₙ + I_{n−2} = 1/(n − 1) for n ≥ 2, and find I₄. [6 marks]
6. Iₙ = ∫₀^{π/2} cosⁿx dx. Use the result Iₙ = ((n − 1)/n)I_{n−2} to find I₅. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. dy/dx = −tan x (1). √(1 + tan²x) = sec x (1). ∫₀^{π/4} sec x dx = [ln|sec x + tan x|]₀^{π/4} (2) = ln(1 + √2) (1).
2. (6t)² + (6t²)² = 36t²(1 + t²) (1). s = ∫₀^√3 6t√(1 + t²) dt (1) = [2(1 + t²)^{3/2}]₀^√3 (2) = 16 − 2 = 14 (1).
3. dy/dx = x^{−1/2}, y√(1 + 1/x) = 2√(x + 1) (2). S = 2π∫₀³ 2√(x + 1) dx (1) = 4π × (2/3)[(x + 1)^{3/2}]₀³ (1) = (8π/3)(8 − 1) = 56π/3 (1).
4. Parts: [−xⁿe^{−x}]₀^∞ + n∫x^{n−1}e^{−x} dx (2). Boundary term 0, since xⁿe^{−x} → 0 (1). I₀ = 1 (1). Iₙ = n(n − 1)...1 × I₀ = n! (1).
5. Iₙ + I_{n−2} = ∫ tan^{n−2}x(tan²x + 1) dx = ∫ tan^{n−2}x sec²x dx (2) = [tan^{n−1}x/(n − 1)]₀^{π/4} = 1/(n − 1) (2). I₀ = π/4, I₂ = 1 − π/4, I₄ = ⅓ − I₂ = π/4 − 2/3 (2).
6. I₁ = 1 (1). I₅ = (4/5)(2/3)(1) = 8/15 (1).
